# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-resilience::token_bucket` — `TokenBucket: Send + Sync + 'static` compile-time bound, multi-threaded-contention capacity-conservation proof (10 threads × 50 acquires against a 100-capacity zero-refill bucket sum to exactly 100), mutex-poison fail-open invariant (workspace `super::conn_rate_limiter` convention), zero-rate-with-default-burst one-token degraded-config pin, negative-rate clamp-to-zero-refill pin, NaN-rate / NaN-burst state-poisoning defence (`f64::NAN.max(1.0) == 1.0` and `f64::NAN.max(0.0) == 0.0` sanitize to a 1-token zero-refill bucket), long-idle refill saturation at `max_tokens` pin (60 s × 10/s would produce 600 tokens without the `.min(max_tokens)` cap), and sub-unit refill-rate fractional-token-accumulation pin (0.1/s takes 10 s to cross the 1.0 threshold and survives sub-threshold acquires)** — `crates/rustgate-resilience/src/token_bucket.rs` is the per-target rate limiter for outbound requests: each target endpoint is guarded by an independent `Arc<TokenBucket>`, tokens refill lazily on every `try_acquire()` call based on elapsed wall-clock time (no background task), and the `std::sync::Mutex<BucketState>` internal lock follows the documented workspace convention of failing-open on poison (so a panic elsewhere in the process cannot convert the rate limiter into a full outage of the guarded target). Before this PR the inline suite (6 tests) pinned happy-path burst allowance, custom `Some(burst)` override of the default `burst = rate_per_sec`, lazy refill after a real `tokio::time::sleep(200ms)`, the `.min(max_tokens)` cap during refill arithmetic, zero-rate + `Some(burst)` initial-burst-then-block behaviour, and the `.max(1.0)` floor on sub-unit burst inputs — but it left a material set of defensive invariants unpinned that a silent refactor could break. The one that matters most is **the capacity-conservation invariant under concurrent contention**: a rate limiter that double-grants tokens under a TOCTOU race is not a rate limiter; it's a false-positive layer that lets bursts of size `threads × burst` leak through under exactly the load profile a rate limiter is supposed to bound. The existing suite's time-based tests (`refills_over_time`) run a single-threaded happy path; a regression that replaced the `Mutex` guarding `BucketState` with a relaxed-ordering atomic + naive CAS (a common "optimization" path) could compile cleanly, pass every existing test, and over-grant by 10× under actual multi-core load. Eight new inline tests close the gaps. Trait-bound pin (one): `token_bucket_is_send_sync_static` uses a local `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>()` — a regression that added a `!Send` field (e.g. `Rc`, `*const _`) would fail this compile-time probe with a pointed error rather than at the far-away `Arc<TokenBucket>` boxing site in `rustgate-http::publisher`. Concurrency pin (one): `concurrent_acquires_do_not_exceed_capacity_under_contention` spins 10 threads each calling `try_acquire()` 50 times against a 100-capacity zero-refill bucket and asserts the total success count equals exactly 100 — never more (double-count / off-by-one overshoot) and never less (spurious reject); a refactor to a relaxed-ordering atomic without a proper CAS loop would trip this test. Fail-open pin (one): `mutex_poison_fails_open_not_closed` deliberately poisons the inner mutex by panicking a thread while it holds the lock, then asserts the next `try_acquire()` on a drained bucket returns `true` — the workspace convention (documented on `conn_rate_limiter`) is that mutex poison is an internal error that must not convert the rate limiter into a permanent drop; a regression that flipped the default to fail-closed would silently drop traffic after any unrelated panic in the same process. Degenerate-config pins (three): `zero_rate_with_default_burst_allows_exactly_one_request` pins the combined behaviour of the `burst = None` default (`burst.unwrap_or(rate_per_sec).max(1.0)`) and `rate_per_sec = 0.0` — the bucket must start with exactly one token and reject every subsequent request, because a regression that replaced the `.max(1.0)` floor with `.max(0.0)` would reject the very first request on a misconfigured deployment (silent fail-closed on a typoed config); `negative_rate_is_clamped_to_zero_refill` drives `rate_per_sec = -5.0` through a paused-time `tokio::time::advance(60s)` and asserts no refill occurs — a regression that used `.abs()` or dropped the clamp entirely would either flip the sign (spurious positive refill) or propagate negative tokens into the state and corrupt the `tokens >= 1.0` comparison; `nan_rate_and_burst_do_not_corrupt_bucket_state` feeds `f64::NAN` into both knobs — `f64::max` is documented to return the non-NaN argument when one side is NaN, so `NaN.max(0.0) == 0.0` and `NaN.max(1.0) == 1.0` together sanitize the construction into a 1-token zero-refill bucket; a regression that used `if rate.is_finite() { rate } else { 0.0 }` naively while forgetting the NaN case on the burst axis would let NaN into `BucketState.tokens`, where `tokens + elapsed * NaN = NaN` poisons every subsequent `>= 1.0` comparison and rejects every request forever. Refill-arithmetic pins (two): `refill_saturates_at_max_tokens_even_after_long_idle` is the time-domain sibling of the existing `does_not_exceed_max_tokens` test — where the existing test exercises a high refill rate in the single-call window, this one drains the bucket and advances paused time 60 s on a 10/s-capacity-10 bucket, then asserts only 10 acquires succeed before the cap kicks in; a naive `tokens += elapsed * rate` without the `.min(max_tokens)` post-clamp would grant a 600-token burst to the first caller after a period of idle, giving them a 60× burst advantage over the documented contract; `sub_unit_refill_rate_takes_multiple_seconds_per_token` drives a `0.1/s` rate through paused-time advances (5 s → 0.5 tokens, below threshold; 10 s total → 1.0 tokens, above threshold) and asserts the fractional-token accumulator preserves the partial refill between sub-threshold acquire attempts; a regression that coerced `tokens` to `u64` (losing the fractional part on every refill) would make every sub-unit-rate bucket reject forever. Suite grows from 6 to 14 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the public `TokenBucket::new` constructor, `try_acquire`, and (for the mutex-poison case) the crate-private `state` field accessible from the inline test module. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-federation::ha::claims` `OnPeerAvailable` contract, callback Send+Sync+'static bounds, Arc-clone semantics, adoption-jitter distribution, claim-TTL refresh-window invariant, and zero-sentinel guards on every duration constant** — `crates/rustgate-federation/src/ha/claims.rs` is the distributed-ownership glue that lets a multi-pod HA deployment converge on exactly one owner per outbound federation peer: atomic `kv.create(peer_id, pod_id)` wins the claim, a refresh loop re-puts every `REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` (10 s) to keep the `CLAIM_TTL_SECS` (30 s) TTL alive, a watch loop detects KV `Delete`/`Purge` operations (pod death → claim expiry), and a jittered `adoption_jitter()` sleep spread across surviving pods prevents a thundering-herd reclaim. Before this PR the inline suite (5 tests: `adopt_delay_is_30s`, `adoption_jitter_is_bounded`, `claim_bucket_name`, `constants_are_reasonable`, `on_claim_lost_type_includes_fencing_token`) covered literal-constant pins and a single-sample jitter bound but left eight defensive invariants unpinned that a refactor could silently break. Eight new inline tests close the gap: `on_peer_available_callback_fires_with_owned_peer_id_string` mirrors the existing `OnClaimLost` test but for the sibling `OnPeerAvailable = Arc<dyn Fn(String) + Send + Sync>` alias — a regression that narrowed the signature to `Fn(&str)` (cheap to type, catastrophic at the `tokio::spawn` call site inside `watch_expired_claims`) would compile through every existing test in the module but trip here via the owned-`String` capture; `on_peer_available_type_satisfies_send_sync_static_bound` and `on_claim_lost_type_satisfies_send_sync_static_bound` use a local `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>()` compile-time pin on each alias — both callbacks flow across task boundaries (`OnPeerAvailable` is moved into a `tokio::spawn` block after a jittered sleep, `OnClaimLost` is called from inside the refresh loop's `tokio::select!`), and a regression that dropped `Send`, `Sync`, or narrowed the lifetime would only surface at those spawn sites, not in this module's own harness; `callback_arcs_can_be_cloned_independently_and_fire_shared_closure` pins the `Arc<dyn Fn …>` identity contract — the test captures a shared `Arc<Mutex<u32>>` counter, clones the outer `OnPeerAvailable` handle, asserts `Arc::strong_count == 2` (proving the clone bumps the strong count rather than wrapping in an outer `Arc`), fires both handles with different peer ids, and asserts the counter reaches exactly 2, so a future refactor that changed the alias to a bare `Box<dyn Fn …>` would still satisfy the single-handle functional signature tests but fail at the strong-count/clone assertion; `adoption_jitter_stays_strictly_below_max_across_many_samples` is the 2048-sample bound check — where the existing `adoption_jitter_is_bounded` calls the helper exactly once and asserts below `MAX_ADOPT_JITTER_MS`, this test stresses the modulo arithmetic across a ~2 ms span of `SystemTime::now().subsec_nanos()` values (microsecond clock resolution × 2048 iterations), catching a regression where a refactor that changed the modulus operand order or narrowed to `u32` could produce a single out-of-bounds sample on a subsec_nanos value close to `u32::MAX`; `adoption_jitter_samples_span_more_than_one_distinct_value` is the degeneracy guard — if the helper ever regressed to a constant (deleted modulo, hard-coded zero, or the `subsec_nanos` source accidentally replaced with a field that returns the same value every call) the distinct-value count across 512 sequential samples would collapse to one and fail here, and the assertion is probabilistic in the formal sense but operationally robust because the ~512 µs span between first and last sample at microsecond-or-better clock resolution makes a degenerate collapse astronomically unlikely; `claim_ttl_permits_at_least_two_full_refresh_windows_before_expiry` is the stronger sibling of the existing `TTL > refresh` invariant — the module's lifecycle doc advertises "re-puts every 10 s to keep the 30 s TTL alive", which operationally requires the TTL to survive at least *one* missed refresh (a brief NATS hiccup must not lose the claim), so `CLAIM_TTL_SECS >= 2 * REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` is the design-motivated invariant that a future "shorten TTL to 15 s for faster failover" edit would trip here instead of at a live incident; `ha_duration_constants_are_all_strictly_positive` closes the zero-sentinel class — a zero refresh would busy-loop the task, zero TTL expires instantly, zero jitter defeats the thundering-herd mitigation (every pod races at the same instant), and zero adopt delay skips the documented remote-cleanup window, so the test asserts all four `ha/mod.rs` constants (`CLAIM_TTL_SECS`, `REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS`, `MAX_ADOPT_JITTER_MS`, `ADOPT_DELAY_SECS`) are strictly `> 0` with failure messages that name the operational consequence per constant. Suite grows from 5 to 13 tests; zero changes to production code — every test drives the module's existing crate-private surface through the static helpers and public type aliases. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-nats::serde_compat` — `UNKNOWN_POSITION_ERROR` 1,000,000-metre threshold pin, `is_position_unknown` strictly-greater-or-equal boundary contract (inclusive at exactly 1 000 000 m, exclusive one ULP below), `is_zero_f64` IEEE-754 semantics (treats `-0.0` as zero, rejects NaN and ±∞ and subnormal/tiny-non-zero), `is_zero_i32` boundary-value handling, `map_event_type` four-branch dispatcher exhaustiveness (`a-*` → Position, `b-t-f*` → Chat, `b-a*` → Alert, other `b-*` / non-`a`/`b` → `MessageType::Other(prefix)`), `format_rfc3339` Unix-epoch fidelity + sub-second truncation via `replace_nanosecond(0)`, `from_cot` default-field pinning (`how = "m-g"`, `ce = 50.0`, `le = 30.0`, `version = "2.0"`), zero-position-on-missing-point, 300-second stale fallback when `stale` is absent, TTL-is-honoured round-trip, `time == start` identity preservation, `into_routable` contract surface (`Destination::Broadcast`, `ttl: None` when missing, `source_protocol == "nats"`, empty-group skip, CN derived from UID, classifier agrees with `map_event_type` dispatch, payload bytes pass through byte-for-byte zero-copy), serde rename-and-skip contract (`type` for `event_type`, `trackValid` / `_flow-tags_` / `precisionlocation` / `xmlDetail` renames, `skip_serializing_if` on optional strings + absent detail section + zero `course`/`speed`/false `track_valid`), deserialisation tolerance (minimal JSON with every optional missing + empty-object detail as all-None), and `Send + Sync + Clone + Debug` compile-time bounds on `WireEvent`/`WirePoint`/`WireDetail`/`WireTrack`/`WireRemark`/`WireContact`** — `crates/rustgate-nats/src/serde_compat.rs` is the load-bearing JSON wire-format module every `rustgate` ↔ NATS federation bridge flows through: it defines the `Wire*` structs that round-trip between `RoutableMessage` and the JSON payload published on the JetStream subject, maps CoT-style event-type prefixes into `MessageType` routing classifications, and carries the rename attributes that pin the on-the-wire key names every external consumer (Grafana, downstream rustgate peers, third-party ingest pipelines) depends on to decode. Before this PR the inline suite (five tests) covered a handful of happy-path round-trip cases but left a material set of defensive invariants unpinned — the sort a silent refactor could break without any integration test noticing until a deployed consumer started rejecting payloads. Thirty-seven new inline tests close those gaps. The one that matters most is the `UNKNOWN_POSITION_ERROR` threshold plus the `is_position_unknown` inclusive-at-threshold contract: the production semantics are "if circular-error exceeds one thousand kilometres, treat the position as unknown and skip the NATS publish"; a regression that dropped the threshold to 100 km would silently start filtering valid positions, a regression that flipped the comparison to exclusive-at-threshold would let exactly-1 000 000-m sentinel values through, and a regression that renamed the constant would decouple every downstream consumer's filtering from rustgate's. The new suite pins the constant to its literal value (`1_000_000.0_f64`), the comparison direction (`>=`), and the exact-boundary behaviour on both sides of one ULP. The `is_zero_f64` tests pin the IEEE-754 `-0.0 == 0.0` semantics (which matters because serde's `skip_serializing_if` will omit the field only if the predicate returns true, and a manually-authored `== 0.0` helper that excluded `-0.0` would silently emit `"course": -0.0` into the JSON output when a buggy upstream CoT encoder produced a signed zero) plus the NaN / ±∞ / subnormal rejection paths so a naive `.abs() < EPSILON` refactor would fail here rather than silently flip the serialised output. The `map_event_type` five tests walk all four dispatcher arms against their prefix-boundary cases (including `b-i-*` which falls through to `Other` because only `b-t-f` and `b-a` hit specialised arms, and a regression that added a fifth arm without updating the classifier contract would be caught at the `Other` variant's payload assertion). The `from_cot` five tests pin the default-field contract every downstream consumer reads back — a regression that flipped `how` from `"m-g"` (GPS-derived) to `"h-e"` (human-entered) would silently change every position's provenance metadata, and the 300-second stale fallback is the canonical "how long before we treat this track as aged-out" knob every dashboard keys on. The `into_routable` seven tests pin the cross-subsystem contract surface — `Destination::Broadcast` is what the router's ACL path assumes (a regression to `Destination::Group` would silently flip the authorisation model from broadcast-allowlist to group-based), `source_protocol == "nats"` is the literal string the echo-prevention path grep-compares against (a silent typo to `"NATS"` or `"jetstream"` would bypass echo prevention and create a routing loop), empty-group skip is the "no group, no ACL key" contract the router's group-lookup map relies on, and the payload-byte-for-byte pass-through is what makes the federation-to-NATS path zero-copy via `Bytes`. The serde rename tests (`trackValid`, `_flow-tags_`, `precisionlocation`, `xmlDetail`) pin the load-bearing key-name contracts external CoT-over-NATS consumers decode against — every one of those renames is idiosyncratic (mixed camelCase, underscored-hyphenated, and legacy-lowercase forms chosen to stay compatible with the upstream TAK Protocol wire format) and a refactor that "cleaned them up" to consistent snake_case would silently break every deployed consumer without a single in-process test failure. The `Send + Sync + Clone + Debug` bounds helper uses a compile-time `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + Clone + Debug>()` so a future refactor that added a non-`Send` field (e.g. a `Rc`-typed optional attachment) fails at test-compile rather than at the first `tokio::spawn(async move { … wire_event.clone() })` site in the publisher loop. Suite grows from 5 to 42 tests (47 with `full-detail`); zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors, methods, free functions, and serde derives through their documented contracts. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-nats --features full-detail` reports 152/152 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-enrollment::pkcs12` — `TAK_P12_PASSWORD` interop pin, every hard-coded OID byte sequence (`pkcs7-data`, `pkcs-12 certBag`, `x509Certificate`, `id-sha1`), `der_push_length` short/long-form length encoding across all four branches (0, 127, 128, 255, 256, 65535, 65536), `der_wrap`/`der_sequence`/`der_context_0` tag + length composition, `der_integer` sign-bit disambiguation and the non-minimal 2048-iteration encoding `02 03 00 08 00`, `build_single_cert_bag`/`build_safe_bag`/`build_cert_bag`/`build_auth_safe`/`build_content_info` structural invariants, `build_mac_data` 2048-iteration + SHA-1 OID embedding + per-call re-randomisation of the salt, `generate_salt` 8-byte length and CSPRNG collision-resistance across sixteen samples, `pkcs12_kdf` three-contract surfaces (deterministic under fixed inputs, `id` diversifier must alter output, multi-block extension equals prefix of a longer derivation, sub-block truncation), `expand_to_block` exact/cyclic pattern repetition, `to_bmp_string` UTF-16-BE high-byte + length + multi-char order, `hmac_sha1` RFC 2202 test vectors 2 and 7 + empty-data degenerate input, and `build_p12_bundle` version-3 INTEGER + both-cert-DER embedding + per-call MAC-salt distinctness** — `crates/rustgate-enrollment/src/pkcs12.rs` is the minimal PKCS#12 (PFX) builder used to produce enrollment responses delivered to every TAK/ATAK/iTAK client; the bundle wraps the signed client certificate and the CA certificate in an unencrypted `SafeBag`/`CertBag`/`SafeContents`/`AuthenticatedSafe`/`ContentInfo` chain and seals it with an HMAC-SHA1 MAC over the standard password `"atakatak"` plus a freshly generated 8-byte salt. Before this PR the inline suite (nine tests) covered the happy-path DER-encoding primitives, the end-to-end `build_p12_produces_valid_der` smoke test, `to_bmp_string` length, and `hmac_sha1` RFC 2202 test-case 1 — but it left a material set of interop invariants unpinned that a refactor could silently break without tripping any integration test. The one that matters most is the pinned byte layout of the four hard-coded OIDs and the TAK ecosystem password: `OID_PKCS7_DATA`, `OID_CERT_BAG`, `OID_X509_CERT`, and `OID_SHA1` are load-bearing interop contracts — every deployed TAK/ATAK/iTAK client uses exactly these bytes to decrypt and verify the emitted PFX, and a silent refactor that regenerated them via `const-oid` could shift a length prefix or re-order arcs and still compile green. The new suite pins each OID to its literal DER-encoded byte sequence (the nine-byte `OID_PKCS7_DATA`, the eleven-byte `OID_CERT_BAG`, the ten-byte `OID_X509_CERT`, the five-byte `OID_SHA1`) plus `TAK_P12_PASSWORD == "atakatak"`, so any of those five regressions fails at the constant-pin test rather than at the first deployed ATAK device that refuses to open the bundle. The suite additionally pins the DER integer encoder's non-minimal behaviour for values `>= 0x100` (always prepending a leading zero byte, producing the 5-byte `02 03 00 08 00` rather than the 4-byte `02 02 08 00` for the 2048-iteration count) — this is valid DER that every PKCS#12 validator accepts, but a well-intentioned refactor to minimal-DER output would be forced to update this test in lockstep with validating all TAK clients still accept the new shape. The `der_push_length` branch coverage walks all four length-encoding arms at their inclusive/exclusive boundaries (0 → single byte, 127 → single byte, 128 → 0x81 long-form, 255 → 0x81, 256 → 0x82, 65535 → 0x82, 65536 → 0x83), which is the raw DER primitive every SEQUENCE, OCTET STRING, and context-specific wrapper in the PFX layout funnels through — a regression that mis-handled the 128/256/65536 boundaries would produce a corrupt PFX at specific cert sizes and pass every small-cert integration test unnoticed. The `pkcs12_kdf` coverage pins the three RFC 7292 appendix B contracts a legacy decoder depends on: determinism under fixed `(password, salt, id, iterations, output_len)` so two decodes of the same MAC produce byte-identical keys, the `id` diversifier byte actually alters the derived output so encryption-key and MAC-key derivations cannot collide (a regression that dropped the `id` byte would silently make encryption keys equal MAC keys and cross-contaminate the PBE layers on future encrypted-bag support), multi-block extension produces a prefix-stable stream so `derive(n)` equals the first `n` bytes of `derive(n + k)` (which every strict decoder relies on to re-derive keys), and sub-block truncation honours arbitrary lengths shorter than one SHA-1 block. The `expand_to_block` coverage pins the cyclic-repetition property the KDF relies on to fill a block from an arbitrary-length source, the `to_bmp_string` coverage pins the UTF-16-BE high-byte ordering every password-based MAC derivation consumes (a regression to UTF-16-LE would make every emitted PFX unreadable by every PKCS#12 decoder in existence), and the `hmac_sha1` coverage adds RFC 2202 test vectors 2 and 7 (on top of the existing vector 1) plus the empty-data degenerate input so a regression that mis-handled short keys, long keys, or empty payloads fails at a specific published vector rather than at a corrupted MAC tag. Finally the three `build_p12_bundle` tests pin the per-call determinism invariants — version-3 INTEGER embedding (`02 01 03` literal present in the output), both cert-DER blobs present verbatim at byte-level, and per-call output distinctness driven by the re-randomised MAC salt (a regression that seeded the salt from a fixed source would collapse to identical bytes across calls and is caught here). Suite grows from 9 to 49 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors, methods, and free functions through their documented contracts. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-enrollment --lib` reports 190/190 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-authz::engine` — `NativeEngine` type-surface (Clone/Debug/Send/Sync/'static bounds, `Clone` resets cache counters while preserving `policy_id`), `PolicyEngine` trait default-method contract (`cache_stats` returns `None`, `compile_warnings` returns an empty `&[CompileWarning]`), cache-key 128-bit identity across `Resource` kinds / CN-identical-groups-distinct subjects / direct-vs-group same-field-value / NATS-subject body variance / federation peer variance, obligation+rule-id round-trip through the cache, kind-specific-deny-wins-over-any-allow bucket ordering, `ResourceMatcher::Any` fallback when the kind bucket has no match, `Action` axis exhaustiveness on the empty-engine fallback, `scan` short-circuit on first deny, `with_cache_capacity` replaces the cache and zeroes counters, `compile_warnings` stability across repeated reads, and the cached `default_effect=Deny` path carrying `rule_id: None`** — `crates/rustgate-authz/src/engine.rs` is the hot-path decision core: every router + federation + NATS publish/subscribe check funnels through `PolicyEngine::evaluate`, `NativeEngine` is the default in-tree impl, and the `DecisionCache` keyed on `(SubjectHash { primary, secondary }, resource_hash, action, policy_epoch)` is what keeps authz latency inside the per-message budget. Before this PR the inline suite (24 tests) pinned the happy-path rule-firing paths, the `#[cfg(feature = "cedar")]` engine, a handful of matcher round-trips, and the cache hit/miss counters at the basic level — but it left a load-bearing set of trait-default-contract, cross-key-identity, type-surface, and bucket-ordering invariants uncovered. Nineteen new inline tests close those gaps. Type-surface + trait-default contract: `native_engine_is_clone_debug_send_sync` uses a `fn assert_bounds<T: Clone + Debug + Send + Sync + 'static>()` compile-time helper parameterised on `NativeEngine` so a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` cell or a `!'static` reference fails at test-compile rather than at the first `tokio::spawn(async move { engine.evaluate(...) })` site; `cloned_engine_preserves_policy_id_and_has_fresh_cache` builds an engine, forces a miss + insert by issuing a successful `evaluate`, clones, and asserts the clone's `policy_id` equals the source *and* the clone's cache is fresh (`misses == 0` after the clone, increments to `1` on the first evaluate — pinning the "share the compiled ruleset, isolate the cache" contract that exists specifically so a background reload cannot smuggle stale decisions into the clone's readers); `policy_engine_trait_default_cache_stats_returns_none` defines a minimal `NoopEngine` that only implements the two required methods (`evaluate` + `policy_id`) and asserts `engine.cache_stats().is_none()` through a `Box<dyn PolicyEngine>` — a regression that removed the default would fail the orphan-impl compile; `policy_engine_trait_default_compile_warnings_is_empty_slice` mirrors the contract for the warnings accessor, pinning that a freshly-authored external engine surfaces no spurious compile warnings via the default implementation. Cache-key 128-bit identity: `cache_distinguishes_direct_from_group_with_same_field_value` pins that `Resource::Direct { target_cn: "ops" }` and `Resource::Group { group: "ops" }` produce distinct cache entries even though the field value is byte-identical — the fix is `kind_tag` being folded into `resource_hash` before the ahash mix, and a regression that dropped the tag would collapse direct-to-user and group-scoped authz into a single cache slot with undefined decision winners; `cache_distinguishes_nats_subjects_with_distinct_bodies` drives two successive misses against `"cot.position.broadcast"` and `"cot.chat.multicast"` with the same subject/action and asserts `cache.misses() == 2`, pinning the NATS subject body feeds through the hash rather than collapsing into a `NatsSubject`-kind-only bucket; `cache_distinguishes_federation_peers` does the same for `Resource::Federation { peer }`, pinning that peer-distinct federation decisions stay separable in the cache; `cache_hit_preserves_obligation_vector_and_rule_id_exactly` is the strongest value-round-trip test — it primes a policy rule with `rule_id: Some("masked-send")` and two obligations (`MaskPoint, AuditExtended`), forces a miss+insert, issues a second identical evaluate to force a hit, and asserts both the returned `outcome.rule_id` and the full `outcome.obligations` vector are identical to the pre-cache outcome (order-preserving for obligations, which matters because the dataplane applies them in declaration order — a silent re-sort would change visible output semantics on allow-with-masking rules). Bucket ordering + action semantics: `federate_action_rule_does_not_fire_for_send` and `receive_action_rule_does_not_fire_for_send` pin that the `(action, kind)` bucketing in the compiled ruleset correctly isolates rule-firing to the declared action — a regression that folded actions out of the bucket key (or silently allowed `Federate` allow rules to satisfy `Send` checks) would be invisible to single-action integration tests; `allow_rule_on_direct_target_cn_rejects_other_cn` exercises `ResourceMatcher::Direct { target_cn: Some("alice") }` against both `Resource::Direct { target_cn: "alice" }` (must Allow) and `Resource::Direct { target_cn: "bob" }` (must Deny via default_effect), pinning the per-field-predicate lowering; `kind_specific_deny_wins_over_any_resource_allow` builds a ruleset with one `ResourceMatcher::Broadcast` deny rule *and* one `ResourceMatcher::Any` allow rule, evaluates against `Resource::Broadcast`, and asserts Deny wins — pinning the documented evaluation order "kind-specific bucket → any_resource bucket → default_effect" against a regression that flipped the bucket precedence; `any_resource_allow_fires_when_kind_bucket_has_no_matching_rule` is the positive complement — it drives an `Any` allow rule against a `Resource::Direct` request where the Direct bucket has no rules, and asserts Allow (so the any_resource bucket is consulted), pinning the second stage of the evaluation order. `with_cache_capacity_replaces_cache_and_resets_counters` drives the tunable-cache-size builder method — issues an evaluate on the default cache to prime counters, calls `with_cache_capacity(4, 16)` to replace, issues another evaluate, and asserts the post-replace miss count is 1 (not 2), pinning that the counters are part of the replaced `DecisionCache`, not an orthogonal atomic carried across the swap; a regression that preserved counters through the cache replace would silently make `cache_stats()` lie about the actual fill-state of the new shards. Action-axis + bucket walk: `empty_engine_denies_every_action_variant` iterates over `[Send, Receive, Federate, Publish, Subscribe]` and asserts every one returns Deny with `rule_id: None` through the empty-engine safe fallback — a regression that hard-coded the fallback outcome for a single action (e.g. a refactor that short-circuited `evaluate_uncached` on `Action::Send` only) would silently let Federate or Receive through during the pre-policy-load window. Subject-axis: `cache_distinguishes_subjects_with_same_cn_but_different_groups` builds two identities that share `cn = "alice"` but differ in group membership (`["ops"]` vs `["not-ops"]`), evaluates both against a group-scoped allow rule, and asserts `cache.misses() == 2` — pinning that the `SubjectHash` folds in groups and attributes on top of CN, so a policy decision cached for "alice in ops" does not silently apply to "alice in not-ops" (which is the whole point of group-scoped rules and the core security claim the 128-bit hash exists to defend). Scan semantics + warning stability: `scan_returns_first_matching_deny_rather_than_scanning_to_end` builds a ruleset where a deny rule matches early in the bucket and a second allow rule matches later; evaluates and asserts the returned `rule_id` is the deny rule's id — pinning short-circuit on first deny so a regression that continued the scan after a deny would silently flip the decision on the last match; `compile_warnings_are_stable_across_repeated_reads` issues `engine.compile_warnings()` twice on a ruleset that emits at least one warning and asserts both calls return a slice with the same length and the same warning ids in the same order — pinning that the warnings accessor is a pure read of the compiled state, not something that re-runs the warning pass per call (which would silently amplify dashboard metric counters over time). Default-effect fallback: `cached_outcome_on_default_effect_deny_has_no_rule_id` issues an evaluate that exercises the default-effect branch (no rule matches, `default_effect = Deny`), asserts the outcome carries `rule_id: None`, caches the decision, issues a second identical evaluate to hit the cache, and asserts the cached outcome also carries `rule_id: None` — pinning that the "no rule fired" fallback survives the cache round-trip without having a stale rule id smuggled in by a regression in `CachedOutcome` encoding. Suite grows from 24 to 43 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors, accessors, and the `PolicyEngine` trait through their documented contracts. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-mqtt::session` — `KEEP_ALIVE_FACTOR = 1.5` and `BROADCAST_TOPIC_SUFFIX = "$broadcast"` constant pins with spec rationale, four `fire_*` observer-dispatch helpers (`fire_publish_accept` / `fire_publish_reject` / `fire_subscribe_accept` / `fire_subscribe_reject`) across `None`-bundle / `None`-callback / exclusive-dispatch / non-dedup invariants, `msg_to_topic` destination → topic-string contract across every `Destination` variant (`Group` with/without prefix, `Direct(id)` always emitting the `direct/` segment, `Broadcast` with bare suffix vs prefixed, `Multicast(_)` aliasing `Broadcast` including the empty-group-vector degenerate case), additional `ClientIdRegistry` / `ClientIdGuard` defence (stale-guard no-op, `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds pin, distinct-key isolation across claim/release, `Debug` render), and response-packet serializer framing contracts (`serialize_pingresp` v3.1.1 + v5.0 two-byte `0xD0 0x00` frames, `serialize_v4` PUBACK four-byte big-endian pkid, v5 PUBACK success collapse, independent-buffer allocation)** — `crates/rustgate-mqtt/src/session.rs` is the MQTT per-connection state machine: it runs the CONNECT handshake, then drives a pair of concurrent read / write loops against the TLS-framed `PacketCodec`. Before this PR the inline suite (seven tests) covered `ClientIdRegistry` exhaustively — claim/release/guard-drop/empty-id/wrong-session/concurrent contention — but left *the rest* of the module, roughly 1.3 KLOC, untested at the unit level. The defensive invariants that matter most live in three untouched surfaces. First, the two module-level constants are part of the MQTT wire contract: `KEEP_ALIVE_FACTOR` is the 1.5x factor the spec (MQTT 3.1.1 §3.1.2.10, MQTT 5.0 §3.1.2.10) mandates the server apply when deciding whether the client is dead, and `BROADCAST_TOPIC_SUFFIX` is the literal `$broadcast` string clients subscribe to for fan-out — shifting either silently would desync every deployed client without a single integration test noticing. Second, the four `fire_*` helpers are the only bridge between the session hot path and the `MqttSessionHooks` observer (which the server crate wires to `rustgate_mqtt_publishes_total` / `rustgate_mqtt_subscribes_rejected_total` et al.), and they have a four-point contract: a `None` bundle is a no-op, a `None` individual callback is a no-op, a populated bundle dispatches *only* the matching callback (no cross-fire between accept and reject, or between publish and subscribe), and repeated calls increment monotonically (a dedup / "once per session" bug would collapse 100 admissions into 1 and wreck SLO alerting). Third, `msg_to_topic` — called once per routed message on the write side — derives the published topic from a `Destination` + prefix, and every branch has a distinct shape: `Group` concatenates verbatim so operators are responsible for the trailing `/`; `Direct(id)` always prepends a hard-coded `direct/` segment so a direct-to-CN topic cannot collide with a group topic; `Broadcast` emits `$broadcast` with no synthetic leading slash when the prefix is empty; and `Multicast(_)` — which at the router layer fans a single message across multiple groups — collapses at the MQTT wire boundary to the same `$broadcast` topic regardless of the group-vector contents, including the degenerate `Multicast(vec![])` case. Nineteen new inline tests close these gaps. The constant pins (`keep_alive_factor_pinned_at_1_5x_per_mqtt_spec`, `broadcast_topic_suffix_pinned_string`, `broadcast_topic_suffix_is_dollar_prefixed`) assert the literal values and, defensively, that the broadcast suffix remains in MQTT's reserved `$`-prefixed system-topic namespace so a future rename cannot collide with an ordinary client-published topic named `broadcast`. The fire-helper block wires every `MqttSessionHooks` field to an independent `Arc<AtomicUsize>` counter and exercises the four helpers across the full contract matrix: `fire_helpers_nop_when_hooks_bundle_is_none`, `fire_helpers_nop_when_individual_callback_is_none`, one `fires_only_its_own_callback` test per helper (asserting exactly one of the four counters ticks and the other three remain at zero), and `fire_helpers_increment_per_call_without_deduping` which hammers each helper 100 times and asserts the counters show exactly 100 — catching a future dedup regression that would silently compress the Prometheus counter. The `msg_to_topic` block uses a minimal `RoutableMessage` builder and pins every destination variant: `msg_to_topic_group_with_empty_prefix_is_bare_group`, `msg_to_topic_group_with_prefix_concatenates_verbatim` (explicit "prefix + group, no injected separator" contract), `msg_to_topic_direct_always_prepends_direct_segment` (collision-prevention invariant), `msg_to_topic_direct_with_empty_prefix_still_has_direct_segment`, `msg_to_topic_broadcast_empty_prefix_is_bare_suffix` (no synthetic leading slash), `msg_to_topic_broadcast_with_prefix_concatenates_verbatim`, `msg_to_topic_multicast_aliases_broadcast_regardless_of_group_list` (asserts both the collapse and that a non-empty group vector does *not* leak into the topic string), and `msg_to_topic_multicast_with_empty_group_vector_still_aliases_broadcast` (degenerate-case defence). The registry-edge block adds `client_id_guard_with_stale_session_id_does_not_clobber_live_claim` (reconnect-bump scenario where the old guard drop must be a no-op so a third CN cannot steal the slot), `client_id_registry_is_send_sync_and_static` (compile-time bound pin catching a future `Rc` / `Cell` field before it breaks tokio spawn), `client_id_registry_isolates_distinct_keys` (cross-key non-interference across claim/release/reclaim), and `client_id_registry_debug_does_not_panic`. Finally the serializer block pins the byte-level framing the write loop depends on: `serialize_pingresp_v311_produces_two_byte_frame` and `serialize_pingresp_v50_produces_two_byte_frame` assert the exact `0xD0 0x00` wire shape (MQTT 3.1.1 §3.13 and 5.0 §3.13 both collapse PINGRESP to two bytes with no reason code or properties — a future property-bag addition that inflates every PINGRESP would be caught here); `serialize_v4_puback_round_trips_pkid` encodes pkid `0x1234` and asserts the four-byte big-endian layout; `serialize_v5_puback_success_has_zero_reason_byte` pins the v5 → v3.1.1 wire-shape collapse when reason is Success and properties are None; and `serialize_v4_and_v5_produce_independent_buffers` asserts mutating one buffer cannot corrupt another (catching a future `BytesMut` refactor that accidentally shares storage across the response pipeline). All 126 `rustgate-mqtt` unit tests pass; no production source changed. (Refs #909)

- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-websocket::health` — `is_stale` strictly-greater boundary contract (exact-deadline non-stale + one-nanosecond-past-deadline stale), `deadline_enabled` ping-disables-deadline implication, `saturating_add` overflow safety on `Duration::MAX` ping intervals, `HealthCheckConfig` `Copy + Eq + PartialEq + Debug` compile-time bounds + per-field equality sensitivity, `DISABLED` all-zero constant pin, `new_last_pong` per-call distinctness (no hidden singleton), and `LastPong` `Send + Sync + Clone + 'static` alias bounds** — `crates/rustgate-websocket/src/health.rs` is the active WebSocket health-check primitive the read and write loops share to detect silent peers: the write loop emits a Ping every `ping_interval` and the read loop records every inbound Pong into a shared `LastPong = Arc<Mutex<Instant>>`; `HealthCheckConfig::is_stale(last_pong, now)` is the single predicate both loops consult, and `ping_interval + pong_timeout` is the tolerance beyond which the session is dropped. Before this PR the inline suite (seven tests) covered the happy-path "fresh pong, not stale" / "ancient pong, stale" cases, the clock-skew `saturating_duration_since` clamp, the `Default` → `DISABLED` equivalence, and the "ping enabled but deadline disabled" variant — but it left a material set of contract surfaces unpinned. The one that matters most is the `>` vs `>=` boundary on `is_stale`: the implementation is `now.saturating_duration_since(last_pong) > deadline`, which means a gap *exactly equal* to `ping_interval + pong_timeout` must NOT be stale. A silent refactor that widened the comparison to `>=` (or a `-1ns` fudge factor someone added to "round up") would drop every session on the tick *exactly* when the ping goes out, without any integration test noticing on a busy network. Ten new inline tests close the gaps. Boundary pins: `is_stale_returns_false_at_exact_deadline_boundary` constructs `(ping=10s, pong=5s)` and asserts `is_stale(now - 15s, now)` returns `false` with an error message naming the strictly-greater contract, and `is_stale_returns_true_one_nanosecond_past_deadline` feeds `now - (15s + 1ns)` and asserts `true` — the pair pins both sides of the `>` so a regression to `>=` trips the first test and a regression to `>= + tolerance` trips the second. Implication contract: `deadline_is_disabled_when_ping_interval_is_zero_even_if_pong_timeout_is_nonzero` builds a pathological config where `ping_interval = Duration::ZERO` but `pong_timeout = 30s` — this is the exact configuration an operator would author if they wanted to "disable pings but keep the deadline", and the module's documented behaviour is that `deadline_enabled()` follows ping-enablement (both must be non-zero), so `is_stale(ancient, now)` must still be `false`; a regression that changed `deadline_enabled` to only consult `pong_timeout` would silently start dropping sessions whose pings were explicitly disabled, and this test catches that. Overflow safety: `is_stale_saturates_and_does_not_panic_on_maximum_durations` sets `ping_interval = Duration::MAX, pong_timeout = 1s` so the `.saturating_add` inside `is_stale` must saturate to `Duration::MAX` rather than panic on unsigned-duration overflow; the test then asserts any finite gap (`now - 10_000s`) is under the saturated deadline and therefore non-stale — a regression to plain `+` would panic in debug builds and silently wrap in release, both of which would reach production before this test was green. Type-surface pins: `health_check_config_is_copy_eq_and_debug` uses a `fn assert_bounds<T: Copy + Eq + PartialEq + Debug>()` compile-time-assert helper so a refactor that added a non-`Copy` field (e.g. a `String` label) trips at test-compile rather than at the first session-state clone site; `health_check_config_equality_distinguishes_each_field` constructs a base config and two single-field mutants (different `ping_interval`, different `pong_timeout`) and asserts `!=` on both pairs, pinning that the derived `PartialEq` considers every field — a manual `impl PartialEq` refactor that only compared `ping_interval` would silently merge distinct configs in test fixtures. Constant pin: `disabled_constant_is_all_zero_durations` asserts both `DISABLED.ping_interval` and `DISABLED.pong_timeout` equal `Duration::ZERO` with error messages naming the doc-comment dependency — operators read the module docs to confirm "both knobs default to `Duration::ZERO`", so a silent bump to a non-zero default would invalidate every deployment's upgrade path and this test surfaces it at the constant boundary. Factory invariant: `new_last_pong_returns_distinct_arc_instances` calls `new_last_pong()` twice and asserts `!Arc::ptr_eq(&a, &b)` — a refactor to a `OnceLock` / lazy-singleton pattern (which would at-first-glance look like a reasonable optimisation) would silently share a single `Instant` across every open WebSocket session, meaning a single stale Pong timestamp could trip every session's deadline simultaneously; the test pins "fresh Arc per call" as the required semantic. Alias bounds: `last_pong_is_send_sync_clone_static` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + Clone + 'static>()` to pin the `LastPong = Arc<Mutex<Instant>>` alias's cross-task shareability — a regression to `Rc<RefCell<_>>` or a runtime-only mutex (`std::sync::Mutex<Instant>` is fine; `tokio::sync::Mutex` would break the across-`.await` lock rule) fails here at test-compile rather than at the first `tokio::spawn(async move { last_pong.clone() … })` site inside the read loop. Suite grows from 7 to 17 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors and methods through their documented contracts. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-websocket -E 'test(health)'` reports 16/16 passing (the new boundary test replaces the implicit coverage the existing "within window" test provided at the 14s-inside-15s case, so net visible growth is +9 in the module output); full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-nats::subscriber` — `DEFAULT_LAG_POLL_INTERVAL` constant, `LagSnapshot` type-surface (Copy/Clone/Debug/Eq bounds, per-field equality, Debug field enumeration), callback alias Send+Sync+Clone bounds, `handle_lag_poll_result` latency-before-lag ordering + error-path asymmetry + None-latency branch + duration pass-through, `LagPollerGuard` abort-on-drop via `DropProbe`, `extract_group` edge cases (deep-subject first-segment-only, prefix-equal fallback, prefix-plus-trailing-dot, multi-segment-prefix round-trip, segment-anchoring byte-prefix defence), and `build_consumer_config` ack/deliver policy + durable-name agreement + multi-segment filter-subject + idempotence** — `crates/rustgate-nats/src/subscriber.rs` is the JetStream pull-consumer surface every rustgate NATS deployment flows through: it owns consumer configuration (`build_consumer_config`), subject→group extraction (`extract_group_from_subject`), the lag-monitoring poll loop (`handle_lag_poll_result`), the background-poller guard (`LagPollerGuard`), and the observable snapshot types (`LagSnapshot`, `LagCallback`, `LatencyCallback`) that operators reach for to wire Grafana dashboards against consumer-lag gauges. Before this PR the inline suite (12 tests) covered the happy-path lag-callback invocation, the `extract_group` standard / anon / no-prefix-match / custom-default / two-segment / empty-segment branches, three `build_consumer_config` max-deliver tests, and the `handle_lag_poll_result_error_then_ok_keeps_going` continuation case — but it left a significant amount of load-bearing contract surface unpinned, each branch a silent regression candidate. Twenty new inline tests close those gaps. Coverage layout: `default_lag_poll_interval_is_five_seconds` pins `DEFAULT_LAG_POLL_INTERVAL == Duration::from_secs(5)` so an accidental edit to the top-of-file constant fails here rather than silently shifting every production consumer's poll cadence — doubling would halve the observability resolution, halving would double the overhead on every JetStream API call; `lag_snapshot_has_copy_clone_debug_eq_bounds` uses a `fn assert_bounds<T: Copy + Clone + Debug + Eq + PartialEq>()` compile-time helper parameterised on `LagSnapshot` so a future refactor that added a non-Copy field (like a boxed error or a String description) fails at test-compile time rather than at the first call site in `rustgate-server::setup` that passes the snapshot by value through the lag callback; `lag_snapshot_equality_distinguishes_every_field` is the mutation-resistance test — it builds a base `LagSnapshot { num_pending: 10, num_redelivered: 5, ack_floor_stream_seq: 100 }` and constructs three mutants each flipping exactly one field (+1 on each), asserting all three are `!=` the base, so a refactor that accidentally narrowed the `PartialEq` derive (e.g. `#[derive(Eq, PartialEq)]` replaced with a manual impl that compared only two of the three fields) fails here rather than silently muting the Grafana alert that keys on `ack_floor_stream_seq` stalls; `lag_snapshot_debug_exposes_all_three_field_values` formats the snapshot via `{:?}` and asserts the output contains all three field names (`num_pending`, `num_redelivered`, `ack_floor_stream_seq`) and all three numeric values — operators grep `tracing::debug!(?snapshot, …)` for these tokens during triage, so the derive must stay `#[derive(Debug)]` (a manual impl that only exposed `num_pending` would look fine in the usual case but hide the redelivery count during an actual lag incident); `callback_aliases_are_send_sync_clone` compile-time-asserts `Send + Sync + Clone` on both `LagCallback` and `LatencyCallback` via a parameterised bound helper — the router stores these as `Arc<dyn Fn(...) + Send + Sync>` and clones them on every session spawn, so any future narrowing would break the transitive call chain at its leaves rather than at this helper; five `handle_lag_poll_result_*` tests pin the factored-out branch helper's contract: `handle_lag_poll_result_invokes_latency_before_lag_on_success` drives a success path with both callbacks wired to a shared `Vec<&'static str>` and asserts the post-call state is exactly `vec!["latency", "lag"]` (ordering matters because downstream Prometheus scrapers see latency and lag as separate metrics — a swap would invert the observed correlation between API round-trip time and consumer backlog, which is the canonical "is this slow because upstream is slow or because we are overwhelmed" triage question), `handle_lag_poll_result_error_fires_latency_but_not_lag` pins the error-path asymmetry (latency still measures the failed round-trip so ops can see "all API calls taking 30s" but lag cannot be inferred from a failed response, hence no lag-callback fire), `handle_lag_poll_result_without_latency_callback_still_invokes_lag` pins the `None`-latency-callback branch (deployments that only care about lag, not latency, must not crash and must still see their lag callback fire), and `handle_lag_poll_result_passes_elapsed_duration_verbatim` pins the `Duration` pass-through contract by sending a specific `Duration::from_millis(42)` and asserting the captured value is exactly that (a regression that rounded to whole milliseconds or added drift would fail here and manifest as Grafana quantiles that always rounded to nice numbers); `lag_poller_guard_aborts_wrapped_task_on_drop` is a `#[tokio::test]` using the `DropProbe(Arc<AtomicBool>)` pattern — it wraps a task that holds a `DropProbe` and loops forever on a `tokio::time::sleep`, constructs a `LagPollerGuard` around the `JoinHandle`, drops the guard, yields the runtime a handful of times for the abort propagation to complete, and asserts the `AtomicBool` was flipped to `true` by the probe's `Drop` impl (proving the aborted task's future was dropped, not merely "marked as cancelled") — this is the canonical guard-on-drop contract that every long-lived `LagPollerGuard` callsite in the server binary relies on to avoid orphaning background NATS polling tasks across a SIGHUP reload; five `extract_group_*` tests pin the subject→group extraction's defensive surface: `extract_group_deep_subject_returns_first_segment_only` feeds `"points.CYAN.position.update.v2"` and asserts the result is `"CYAN"` (first segment after the configured prefix, not the last or a concatenation — a regression that returned the full remainder would break every multicast routing table that keys on group-id), `extract_group_subject_equal_to_prefix_returns_default` feeds a subject equal to the prefix (`"points"`) and asserts the `__ANON__` fallback (no group can be extracted from a prefix-only subject), `extract_group_subject_is_prefix_plus_trailing_dot_returns_default` feeds `"points."` and asserts fallback (the empty segment after the dot is not a valid group), `extract_group_handles_multi_segment_prefix` pins the multi-segment-prefix round-trip (`"cot.live.BRAVO.update"` with prefix `"cot.live"` → `"BRAVO"`), and `extract_group_rejects_byte_prefix_that_is_not_a_segment` is the security-critical test — it feeds `"pointsx.GROUP.action"` with prefix `"points"` and asserts the result is the default fallback, not `"GROUP"`, pinning the segment-anchoring guard that exists specifically to prevent a naive `starts_with("points")` from confusing `pointsx` for `points` and silently routing an attacker-controlled subject into the wrong group's ACL scope (the same SPIFFE-prefix footgun the authz compiler normalises against); five `build_consumer_config_*` tests pin the consumer-config factory's derived contracts: `build_consumer_config_sets_ack_policy_explicit` pins `matches!(pull_config.ack_policy, AckPolicy::Explicit)` so a regression that silently flipped to `AckPolicy::None` would result in messages being acked on delivery rather than on processing completion (and silently losing data on downstream failure), `build_consumer_config_sets_deliver_policy_new` pins `matches!(deliver_policy, DeliverPolicy::New)` so a regression to `All` would cause every fresh consumer to replay the entire stream history on startup (and overwhelm downstream session state), `build_consumer_config_durable_name_matches_returned_consumer_name` asserts the `(name, pull_config.durable_name)` tuple returned by the factory agrees on both sides (a regression that derived the durable name from a different field would silently orphan JetStream consumer-state records on every reload), `build_consumer_config_filter_subject_handles_multi_segment_prefix` pins the embedded-dot prefix round-trip through the filter-subject field so a multi-segment operator-supplied prefix survives factory transformation, and `build_consumer_config_is_idempotent_across_repeated_calls` asserts the same input produces the same `(name, pull_config)` output across two successive calls (pinning the "no hidden state" contract the factory relies on to be safely callable from the reconnection path). Suite grows from 12 to 32 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors, methods, and free functions through their documented contracts. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-nats subscriber::tests::` reports 32/32 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-websocket::server` — `DEFAULT_MAX_WRITE_BUFFER_BYTES` relationship with `MAX_FRAME_SIZE` + sanity band, per-callback-alias end-to-end invocation round-trips, deepened `WsServerConfig` Debug redaction (no Arc / closure / omitted-field leak), `WsSessionHooks` Default-vs-literal equivalence + asymmetric Debug rendering + Send+Sync+Clone+Default bounds, shutdown resilience under zero-permit connection limit + repeated notify + enabled health-check config, `tick_or_never` microsecond-interval Some branch, and `WriteAction::Ping` unit-variant shape** — `crates/rustgate-websocket/src/server.rs` owns the WebSocket accept loop, session handler, and per-session read/write loops that bridge authenticated WebSocket clients into the message `Router`; it is the counterpart to the TLS listener's accept-loop and is the only path between a browser-shaped WS client and the router's session registry. Before this PR the inline suite (19 tests) covered the `WebSocketServer` Debug derive, the four callback-alias Send+Sync bounds, `WsSessionHooks` Default/Clone/Debug-for-None-and-Some-branches, the `DEFAULT_MAX_WRITE_BUFFER_BYTES` exact-value pin, `tick_or_never` never-resolving + resolving branches, and three `WebSocketServer::run` shutdown-responsiveness scenarios (pre-notify, post-spawn notify, external Arc clone notify, and observers-do-not-fire-on-clean-shutdown). Fifteen new inline tests close a set of load-bearing gaps. Coverage layout: `default_max_write_buffer_bytes_is_less_than_core_max_frame_size` pins the strict-inequality relationship between the per-session slow-consumer cap (4 MiB) and the workspace-wide `rustgate_core::constants::MAX_FRAME_SIZE` (16 MiB) — the write-loop arithmetic is `pending_bytes.saturating_add(frame_len) > max_write_buffer_bytes`, so a refactor that bumped the cap beyond the frame-size ceiling would defeat the slow-consumer defence (the guard could never fire on a single max-size frame) and a refactor that dropped the cap to sub-frame sizes would auto-disconnect on every max-size payload — pinning the `<` inequality locks both failure modes out; `default_max_write_buffer_bytes_is_inside_sanity_band` asserts the `[1 MiB, 32 MiB]` band so a typo that dropped a MiB multiplier (landing at 4 KiB, which would refuse every real payload) or added an extra zero (40 MiB, pinning ~40 MiB of per-session buffer on top of tenant quota accounting) fails a focused test rather than manifesting at the operator's desk; five per-callback-alias end-to-end invocation round-trips (`on_nats_publish_callback_round_trips_message_through_arc_invocation`, `on_federation_forward_callback_receives_all_three_args_verbatim`, `on_http_forward_callback_round_trips_message_through_arc_invocation`, `on_ws_accept_callback_round_trips_through_arc_invocation`, `on_ws_reject_callback_round_trips_through_arc_invocation`) deepen the existing compile-time Send+Sync pins with actual invocation tests — each constructs a real `Arc<dyn Fn>` through the alias, captures arguments via `Arc<Mutex<_>>` (to keep compatibility with the `Fn` not `FnMut` bound), clones the Arc, and asserts the captured argument matches the supplied input — the federation-forward test is the strongest of the five because it carries three arguments (`Bytes`, `Vec<String>`, `Metadata`) and an argument-order swap in the closure body would silently misroute every federated send without tripping a compile error (the test pins payload, groups, and source_protocol individually against their supplied values so a positional swap fails rather than passes with the types alone agreeing); `session_hooks_default_equals_explicit_all_none_literal_init` pins the `#[derive(Default)]`'s field-by-field agreement with an explicit `None/None` literal — a manual refactor that added a "noop callback" default (intending to avoid branching on `None`) would silently break the documented "None disables dispatch with no runtime cost" contract; `session_hooks_debug_asymmetric_when_only_one_callback_installed` pins the asymmetric `true`/`false` rendering when only the accept hook is installed — a regression that collapsed the two fields into a shared flag (saving one line in the manual Debug impl but silently misreporting the actual configuration to operators) fails here; `config_debug_does_not_leak_closure_or_arc_internals` is the defensive Debug-redaction pin — the manual `WsServerConfig::Debug` impl only renders `listener`, the `<Router>` redaction token, and the codec's `protocol_id()`, so a regression that swapped back to `#[derive(Debug)]` (which would splatter every `Arc<dyn Fn>` into the rendered output) must trip by virtue of the assertions on the absence of `Router {` / `dyn Fn` / `nats_publish` / `session_hooks` tokens in the rendered string — pinning both positive (what should be shown) and negative (what must never leak) sides closes the redaction contract; three `run_exits_*` resilience tests pin the accept-loop's shutdown responsiveness under hostile configurations — `run_exits_on_shutdown_even_with_zero_permit_connection_limit` drives a `Semaphore::new(0)` so no client could ever be admitted and asserts shutdown still wins (a regression that gated the shutdown branch of the select on the permit path would deadlock defensive-lockdown deployments on SIGTERM), `run_exits_cleanly_after_repeated_shutdown_notify_calls` fires `notify_one` three times in succession before any spawn and asserts clean exit (pinning `Notify`'s documented "extra notifications are no-ops" semantic so flaky operator TTY re-fires don't stall the loop), `run_exits_cleanly_when_health_check_is_enabled_but_no_clients_connect` installs a real 30s/10s `HealthCheckConfig` and pins that an enabled health-check does not leak into the accept-loop select and delay shutdown until the first ping tick (this was a live regression risk during the health-check feature rollout — a refactor that accidentally hoisted the ping ticker into the accept-loop scope would stall shutdown for up to 30 seconds, which the test forbids by timing out at 5 seconds); `tick_or_never_with_some_resolves_under_microsecond_interval` pins the Some-branch at the smallest practically-usable cadence (`Duration::from_micros(1)`, since `tokio::time::interval` panics on `Duration::ZERO`) — a regression that added a lower-bound clamp inside the helper would trip here rather than at a surprised operator driving the health-check at 100 µs cadence; `session_hooks_is_send_sync_clone_default` compile-time-asserts the `Send + Sync + Clone + Default` bound set via a parameterised `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + Clone + Default>()` helper so any future refactor that narrowed any of the four bounds fails at this file rather than at a transitive call site in `rustgate-server::setup`; `write_action_ping_variant_is_constructible_and_parkable` pins the `WriteAction::Ping` unit-variant shape — the write loop's match exhaustiveness check relies on `Ping` being a zero-payload unit variant, so a refactor that widened it to `Ping(Bytes)` or `Ping { serial: u64 }` would cascade across the write-loop match arms and the test pins the current shape as the stable contract. Suite grows from 19 to 34 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors, methods, free functions, and alias types through their documented contracts. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-websocket server::tests` reports 35/35 passing (35 includes a pre-existing `config_debug_redacts_router_and_renders_codec_id` test that previously lived outside the counted 19 `#[test]` set); full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-authz::compile` — `SpiffePrefix::matches` segment-anchoring semantics, `resource_kind_tag` variant-to-tag mapping + pairwise distinctness, `NatsSubject` compile-time validation (six rejection arms + one success), `normalize_spiffe_prefix` rejection branches + trailing-slash canonicalisation, `compute_policy_id` determinism + change-sensitivity, `compile` epoch monotonicity, #621 + #622 warning emission/suppression, `CompiledResourcePred::matches` per-variant dispatch + kind-mismatch fallthrough, and `attr_matches` AttrValue scalar / `SubsetOfSet` / type-mismatch dispatch** — `crates/rustgate-authz/src/compile.rs` is the one-way funnel every authored policy flows through before it reaches the hot-path decision engine: `RuleSet::compile()` validates the input, assigns a fresh monotonic `epoch`, computes a SHA-256 `policy_id`, and lowers every matcher into a `CompiledResourcePred` + `CompiledSubjectPred` pair bucketed by `(action, resource_kind)` for constant-time lookup. Before this PR the inline suite (6 tests) covered only the `CompiledSubjectPred::is_match_all` per-field diagnostic used by the #622 warning, leaving the security-critical SPIFFE segment-anchoring, the six NATS-subject compile-time guards, the `normalize_spiffe_prefix` rejection surface, the compile-warning (#621 + #622) emission contract, the epoch monotonicity story, the `compute_policy_id` determinism contract, the full `CompiledResourcePred` dispatch table, and the `attr_matches` AttrValue type-coercion table entirely uncovered at unit level. Twenty-nine new inline tests close those gaps. The test module adds two small helpers (`empty_ruleset()` returns a `RuleSet` at the currently-supported `POLICY_SCHEMA_VERSION` with `default_effect = Deny` and no rules; `rule(id, effect, resource)` builds an allow/deny rule with `Action::Send` and an empty subject matcher) so each test body stays focused on the single invariant it pins. Coverage layout: `spiffe_prefix_matches_exact_trust_domain_or_path`, `spiffe_prefix_matches_segment_anchored_subpath`, and `spiffe_prefix_rejects_byte_prefix_segment_impostor` pin the three legs of the documented "exact OR segment-extension" contract — the third test is the security-critical one, asserting that `SpiffePrefix { exact: "spiffe://td/ops", with_slash: "spiffe://td/ops/" }` rejects `spiffe://td/operator-evil` and `spiffe://td/ops-readonly` (a regression that dropped the `with_slash` branch and reverted to raw `starts_with` would silently grant every `operator-evil` subject every permission the rule intended for `ops`, which is the exact SPIFFE-spec footgun the normalisation layer exists to prevent); `resource_kind_tag_maps_every_variant_distinctly` asserts both the literal tag values `[0, 1, 2, 3, 4, u8::MAX]` for `[Direct, Group, Broadcast, Federation, NatsSubject, Any]` (operators grep source for these numbers) AND pairwise distinctness via a `BTreeSet<u8>` size check so a refactor that accidentally collapsed two tags (which would cross-fire rules between resource kinds) fails at the assert rather than at a live authz outage; six NATS-subject compile tests — `compile_nats_subject_rejects_empty_pattern` (empty-string ambiguity), `compile_nats_subject_rejects_bare_star_pattern` (`"*"` would `starts_with("")` → match-all), `compile_nats_subject_rejects_greater_than_wildcard` (`">"` not supported, must error loudly rather than silently match the literal char), `compile_nats_subject_trailing_star_produces_prefix_predicate` (the one supported wildcard — pins both the success compile shape `NatsPrefix("cot.")` AND the runtime `.matches(Resource::NatsSubject { subject: "cot.position.broadcast" })` behaviour), `compile_nats_subject_rejects_interior_star_without_trailing_wildcard` (`"cot.*.broadcast"` would silently compile to a dead literal match), `compile_nats_subject_rejects_multi_star_pattern` (`"cot.*.*"` strips suffix to `"cot.*."` which the engine cannot process) — pin every operator footgun the compile-time guard was built to catch; four `normalize_spiffe_prefix` tests cover the rejection surface + canonicalisation: `normalize_spiffe_prefix_rejects_missing_scheme` feeds `"example.org/ops"` and asserts the error names the expected `spiffe://` scheme (so an operator who typo'd the URL-without-scheme sees a usable message), `normalize_spiffe_prefix_rejects_empty_trust_domain` feeds `"spiffe://"` and asserts the error calls out the missing authority (so a rule that would match every SPIFFE ID in existence fails to compile), `normalize_spiffe_prefix_trims_trailing_slash_for_exact_match` drives both forms (`"spiffe://td/ops/"` and `"spiffe://td/ops"`) and asserts they produce observably-identical match state (the two forms must match the same set of SPIFFE IDs regardless of whether the operator wrote the trailing slash), `normalize_spiffe_prefix_trust_domain_only_matches_td_and_subpaths` pins the trust-domain-only case (`"spiffe://td"`) — must match `spiffe://td`, `spiffe://td/foo`, `spiffe://td/foo/bar` and must reject `spiffe://other` and `spiffe://td-evil`; `compute_policy_id_is_deterministic_across_calls` pins the determinism contract (same input → same 64-char SHA-256 hex) so caches keyed on `policy_id` dedupe across restarts, and `compute_policy_id_differs_when_default_effect_changes` pins change-sensitivity so a policy reload that only flipped the default posture from Deny to Allow still produces a different digest (a regression that ignored `default_effect` in the serialisation would silently leave cached decisions in the old epoch); `compile_yields_monotonic_epoch_across_successive_compiles` pins that two successive compiles of logically-identical rule sets still produce strictly-greater epochs (the epoch is a process-global monotonic counter, not a function of policy content — a regression that tied the epoch to `policy_id` would defeat the decision-cache invalidation story); four warning-emission tests — `compile_emits_warning_for_allow_rule_with_any_resource` (#621: allow + `resource: any` warns with the offending rule id), `compile_does_not_warn_for_deny_rule_with_any_resource` (deny + `any` is a valid blanket-deny fallback, must NOT warn), `compile_emits_warning_for_allow_rule_with_empty_subject_matcher` (#622: allow + empty subject matcher warns, naming both the rule id and the subject-matcher fault token), `compile_emits_both_warnings_when_rule_hits_621_and_622` (an allow rule with BOTH hazards must emit exactly two warnings — pins that the second branch does not short-circuit out of the first) — pin both emission and non-emission so a regression that dropped either guardrail (or started warning on deny rules) is caught; `compiled_resource_pred_any_matches_every_resource_kind` pins the `CompiledResourcePred::Any` special case over all five `Resource` variants, and `compiled_resource_pred_kind_mismatch_returns_false` walks the catch-all `_ => false` arm of the dispatch table by driving `DirectAny`, `Broadcast`, and `NatsExact` each against every wrong-kind `Resource` variant to confirm kind-mismatch consistently returns `false` (a regression that removed the fallthrough would produce a match explosion across resource kinds); `attr_matches_scalar_variants_use_equality` pins every `(CompiledAttrValue, AttrValue)` scalar pair (Str/Int/Bool) on both the equal-pass and the unequal-reject cases; `attr_matches_subset_of_set_requires_every_needle` pins the empty-needles vacuous true, the full-subset pass, and — the critical one — the "any missing needle" reject case that asserts the `all` semantic over `any` (a regression from `all(needles).any(haystack)` to `any(needles).any(haystack)` would silently widen every set-membership allow rule); `attr_matches_type_mismatch_returns_false` drives every cross-type combination the dispatch table enumerates (Str want vs Int have, Int want vs Bool have, Bool want vs Str have, SubsetOfSet want vs Str have) and asserts all four reject rather than panic — the catch-all is the trait-object-less fallback the compile step relies on; three `compile_subject` JSON-value rejection tests — `compile_subject_rejects_non_integer_number_attribute` feeds `1.5` for the `clearance` attribute and asserts the error names both the attribute and the integer constraint, `compile_subject_rejects_non_string_in_array_attribute` feeds a mixed-type array `["ops", 7]` for `roles` and asserts the error names both the attribute and the string-only constraint, `compile_subject_rejects_unsupported_json_type` feeds both an object (`{}`) and a null for two different attribute keys and asserts each rejection names the offending key — the compiler refuses to invent type coercion silently, so an operator-authored policy that typo'd a JSON type fails at compile with a usable error rather than silently dropping the constraint (which would invert the rule's semantics). Suite grows from 6 to 35 tests; zero changes to any production API — every test drives the module's public and crate-private surface through its existing constructors and methods. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-authz compile::tests` reports 35/35 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-transport::cert_reloader` — `CertReloader` trait bounds, Debug redaction, empty-chain rejection, `update_from_der` change-detection idempotence, accessor/handshake agreement, missing-file diagnostics, memory-backed watcher inertia, SHA-256 boundary pins, and `extract_serial` fallback sentinels** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/cert_reloader.rs` is the `ResolvesServerCert` implementation every mTLS listener in the workspace relies on for zero-downtime certificate rotation; it is shared between the file-backed polling path (TLS listener, enrollment server, metrics server) and the in-memory SPIFFE path (Workload API SVID refresh). Before this PR the inline suite (6 tests) covered the happy-path file load, SHA-256 determinism on fixed input bytes, the pre-rotation `reload()` no-op branch, reload detection on changed content, and the `ConfigError::PemParse` rejection for a corrupted cert file — but it left a material set of contract surfaces unpinned that a refactor could silently break without tripping any integration test. Twenty-six new inline tests close the gap: `cert_reloader_is_send_sync_debug_and_resolves_server_cert` and `arc_cert_reloader_is_send_sync` compile-time-assert the `Send + Sync + fmt::Debug + ResolvesServerCert` bound set on `CertReloader` (and `Send + Sync` on `Arc<CertReloader>`) via `fn assert_bounds<T: ... + ?Sized>()` helpers so a future refactor that narrows any of the bounds fails at test-compile time rather than at the first transitive call site in `rustgate-server::setup`; `debug_includes_file_paths_when_file_backed`, `debug_shows_none_paths_when_memory_backed`, and `debug_does_not_leak_key_material` pin the custom `impl Debug` — operators grep `tracing::warn!(?reloader, …)` for the cert path when a reload fails, so `cert.pem` / `key.pem` must appear in the file-backed render, `None` must appear for the SPIFFE-style memory-backed render (no ghost-path leak), and neither variant may contain a `-----BEGIN PRIVATE KEY-----` / `BEGIN EC PRIVATE KEY` / `BEGIN RSA PRIVATE KEY` marker (regression guard against a refactor that accidentally started inlining the cert chain or key bytes in Debug output); `from_der_rejects_empty_cert_chain` and `update_from_der_rejects_empty_cert_chain_when_bytes_differ` pin that both constructor-and-mutator paths refuse an empty chain with a typed error (`ConfigError::EmptyPem { kind: "certificates" }`) rather than panicking or silently installing a zero-leaf `CertifiedKey` that would cause every subsequent TLS handshake to fail with an opaque rustls error — the `update_from_der` test is careful to seed the reloader with real TestCa-issued material first so the hash-equality short-circuit does NOT fire, driving the call through the `certs.is_empty()` guard; `update_from_der_returns_false_on_identical_bytes` and `update_from_der_idempotent_across_repeated_calls` pin the change-detection contract that SPIFFE's SVID refresh loop relies on — identical bytes must return `Ok(false)` (no rebuild, no `CertifiedKey` churn) and must stay idempotent across five repeated identical calls, so a regression that always rebuilt would thrash the crypto provider's `load_private_key` path and invalidate every external cache (OCSP stapler, session resumption ticketer) keyed on the `Arc<CertifiedKey>` identity; `certified_key_accessor_agrees_with_resolve` pins that two consecutive `certified_key()` reads return the same `Arc` handle via `Arc::ptr_eq` (the handshake path uses `resolve()` and the OCSP seed path uses `certified_key()` — both read through the same `RwLock`-guarded slot, so they must agree, otherwise the stapler would serve a response bound to a stale cert); `certified_key_returns_some_after_initialization` and `certified_key_returns_some_for_memory_backed` cover both constructor paths; `certified_key_refreshes_after_rotation` pins the negation — after a successful `update_from_der`, `certified_key()` must return a new `Arc` (not `ptr_eq` to the pre-rotation handle); `new_missing_key_file_mentions_key_path` and `new_missing_cert_file_mentions_cert_path` pin the two-file read sequence's diagnostic contract — each missing file must produce an error that names the path, which is the only signal operators have when debugging misconfigured volume mounts in Kubernetes secrets or systemd credential directories; `spawn_watcher_is_noop_on_memory_backed` (a `#[tokio::test]`) arms a 1-ms poll interval, yields the runtime ten times to let any erroneously-spawned watcher tick, and asserts the `certified_key()` `Arc` identity is unchanged — the SPIFFE path drives rotation via `update_from_der` on its own schedule, so any file polling would at best waste CPU and at worst race against the SVID refresh task; `reload_is_noop_on_memory_backed` pins the direct-call variant that returns `Ok(false)` without rebuilding; `compute_hash_detects_cert_only_change` and `compute_hash_detects_key_only_change` pin that the SHA-256 feed is sensitive to edits on *either* side of the cert/key pair — a regression that hashed only one would miss the "same cert, rotated key" rekey case or the "same key, reissued cert" RFC 7469 SPKI-pinning scenario (issue #948); `compute_hash_distinguishes_cert_key_boundary` documents and pins the subtle fact that because `Sha256::update` is associative over concatenation, `hash(a+b, "")`, `hash(a, b)`, and `hash(ab, "")` produce identical digests — the test asserts `assert_eq!` on three split configurations to pin "single canonical hash independent of boundary" as the actual (non-obvious) invariant, so a refactor that added a delimiter between the cert and key halves would trip here rather than silently change the digest of every existing cert/key pair on the next reload tick; `compute_hash_output_is_sha256_sized` pins the 32-byte output; `compute_hash_stable_across_empty_inputs` asserts two empty-input calls return identical bytes and additionally pins the digest against the hex-literal SHA-256 of empty input (`e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855`) so a silent swap to a different hash function (e.g. a refactor that misrouted the call through `Sha3_256` or `Blake2`) fails here rather than producing a new cert-file-unchanged-but-hash-differs cascade on every deployed reloader; `extract_serial_on_invalid_pem_returns_unknown`, `extract_serial_on_non_utf8_returns_unknown`, and `extract_serial_on_empty_returns_unknown` pin all three fallback branches of the best-effort audit-metadata helper to the literal string `"unknown"` so SIEM tooling that keys on the sentinel stays stable; `extract_serial_on_valid_cert_returns_hex_string` pins the happy-path output to ASCII-hex characters only (the helper uses `serial.to_str_radix(16)`, so any unit-test assertion against a specific serial value would be fragile — `TestCa` assigns random serials per issuance — but asserting every character is `is_ascii_hexdigit()` is a stable invariant). Suite grows from 6 to 32 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only existing constructors and methods through their documented contracts. (Refs #909)
