Five unit tests pin the contract: `build_info_labels_shape` asserts the documented label order so scrapers cannot silently break on a rename, `enabled_features_always_includes_metrics` guards the invariant that the function is only reachable inside a metrics-feature build, `build_info_registered_after_init` pins that `init()` populates the `OnceLock`, `build_info_exposes_pinned_label_tuple_at_one` exercises the `.with_label_values(...)` lookup with the real compile-time strings and asserts the gauge reads `1`, and `build_info_gathered_from_default_registry` walks `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` to confirm the metric is actually reachable on a scrape (label set matches `BUILD_INFO_LABELS` modulo Prometheus's alphabetical ordering, single time series, value 1). No runtime cost — the labels are computed once at startup and never touched again. (Refs #908)
- **MQTT PUBLISH / SUBSCRIBE operation counters** — four new Prometheus `IntCounter`s close the operation-level observability gap left by the connection-level counters: `rustgate_mqtt_publishes_total` fires once per PUBLISH packet after topic validation, prefix mapping, and group-membership authorisation all pass and the message has been handed to `Router::route`; `rustgate_mqtt_publishes_rejected_total` fires once per PUBLISH refused on the session-handler boundary (QoS2 unsupported, topic validation, prefix-mapping, authorisation); `rustgate_mqtt_subscribes_total` and `rustgate_mqtt_subscribes_rejected_total` fire **per filter** (a SUBSCRIBE carrying three filters where two are denied produces two reject and one accept increment), because the MQTT 5 SUBACK shape requires per-filter reason codes and an operator needs to see which filters in a batch were refused rather than averaging the outcome across the packet. Without these four, a dashboard showing `rustgate_mqtt_connections_total` climbing can't distinguish "clients connected but never published" (credential-scope mismatch on the producer) from "clients published but their topics were refused" (ACL misalignment) — both look like `publishes_total` staying flat, but the reject counter tells you which one. The wiring follows the same metrics-agnostic pattern as the connection-level pair: `rustgate-mqtt` gains four new public type aliases `OnMqttPublish`, `OnMqttPublishReject`, `OnMqttSubscribe`, `OnMqttSubscribeReject` (all `Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>`) bundled into a new `MqttSessionHooks` struct that `MqttServerConfig` carries as `session_hooks: Option<MqttSessionHooks>`. Bundling into a struct (rather than four independent `Option<...>` fields) keeps `handle_mqtt_session`'s argument count at 10 instead of 13 without masking the individual hooks at the fire site — the session handlers still pull the specific callback out with `let_chains`. `rustgate-mqtt` **does not** gain a `prometheus` dependency; the `rustgate-server` side wires the four `.inc()` closures under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` and the metric accessors themselves are `#[cfg(feature = "mqtt")]`-gated so a metrics-on-but-mqtt-off release build (the `binary-size` and `check-features --feature-powerset` CI jobs) stays warning-free. Fire-point placement is inline at each early-return site in `handle_publish_v{4,5}` and `handle_subscribe_v{4,5}` rather than classifying a post-hoc `Result`, because a Result-based classifier would conflate business rejection (QoS2 / topic / authz) with infrastructure failure (`BrokenPipe` on `send_response`) and over-count. A helper-per-outcome (`fire_publish_accept` / `fire_publish_reject` / `fire_subscribe_accept` / `fire_subscribe_reject`) keeps the fire pattern uniform so a future handler addition can't forget to fire. Twenty new tests total: seven in `rustgate-mqtt::config` pin the type aliases' `Send + Sync` bounds (compile-time assertion so a refactor that narrowed to `FnMut` would fail test-compile rather than at a transitive caller), the `MqttSessionHooks::default()` all-None shape, the manual `Debug` impl's `Some(<cb>)` / `None` sentinels (callback trait objects do not derive `Debug`, so a `tracing::debug!(?config, …)` site otherwise can't render without leaking a closure pointer), and the `Clone` impl's `Arc`-duplication semantics; thirteen in `rustgate-server::metrics::tests::mqtt_publish_subscribe_counters` pin the counter lifecycle end-to-end (four `*_registered_under_documented_name` assertions confirm the counter name against `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` and bind `.name()` — **not** the deprecated `.get_name()` — along with zero label cardinality, four `*_increments_are_observable` assert `.inc()` is monotonically visible via `> before` — **not** the `int_plus_one`-flagged `>= before + 1`, four `fed_by_mqtt_on_*_callback_end_to_end` tests round-trip the exact `Arc<dyn Fn()>` shape that `setup.rs` installs through the same `MqttSessionHooks`-resident dispatch the session task uses so a silent refactor of either the callback alias or the fire site would fail CI, and one `session_hooks_struct_accepts_all_four_counter_callbacks` builds the full struct end-to-end and drives all four closures so a cross-crate field-rename between `rustgate-mqtt` and `rustgate-server` setup.rs fails at test-compile time). Three existing MQTT integration-test fixtures in `mqtt_integration.rs` gain `session_hooks: None` to keep compilation green. (Refs #908)
- **MQTT listener connection accept/reject counters** — two new Prometheus `IntCounter`s expose the MQTT accept-loop's admission outcomes alongside the existing `rustgate_nats_publishes_total` family: `rustgate_mqtt_connections_total` fires once per accepted TCP connection (after the kernel-level accept succeeds and before the per-session spawn), and `rustgate_mqtt_connections_rejected_total` fires once per rejected connection (accept-time `TcpStream::from_std` failures, which are the only MQTT-listener-level admission failures — TLS handshake failures are scoped to `rustgate_tls_handshake_total` once that metric lands). Without these, operators have no single-signal way to distinguish "no MQTT traffic" from "the listener is healthy but every handshake is failing" — the two most common production causes (cert/CRL mismatch vs. genuine idle) are visually indistinguishable in `rustgate_connections_active` alone. The wire-up follows the same metrics-agnostic pattern the transport and federation crates already use: `rustgate-mqtt` gains two new public type aliases `OnMqttAccept = Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` and `OnMqttReject = Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` plus matching `on_accept: Option<OnMqttAccept>` and `on_reject: Option<OnMqttReject>` fields on `MqttServerConfig`; `rustgate-mqtt` crate **does not** gain a `prometheus` dependency — the transport crate stays protocol-layer-only, and `rustgate-server` composes the callbacks under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` so a metrics-off build drops the counter entirely rather than carrying a no-op `.inc()` call. Because `MqttServerConfig` now carries callback trait objects, `#[derive(Debug)]` is replaced with a manual `Debug` impl that renders the callback fields as `"Some(<cb>)"` or `"None"` sentinels — the same pattern `rustgate-transport` uses for its hot-swap callbacks — so a `tracing::debug!(?config, …)` on the startup path cannot leak a caller-owned closure pointer into logs. The fire-points in `MqttServer::run` are placed at the exact admission boundary: accept callback fires after the `TcpStream::from_std` succeeds but before the `session::spawn` for the connection, and reject callback fires on the same-arm `Err(_)` branch alongside the existing `audit::connection_rejected` emission so the counter and the audit log cannot drift. Ten new tests total: four in `rustgate-mqtt` pin the type aliases (`on_accept_type_is_send_sync_arc_fn`, `on_reject_type_is_send_sync_arc_fn` use the compile-time `is_send_sync_arc_fn::<T>()` assertion helper so a refactor that dropped `Send + Sync` would fail at test-compile time rather than at some transitive caller's integration site) and the `Debug` impl (`debug_none_observers_render_as_none` and `debug_some_observers_render_as_cb_sentinel` wrap `MqttListener::bind` under `#[tokio::test]` because construction requires a live runtime for the underlying `TcpListener::bind` call — the non-async `#[test]` form panics with "there is no reactor running"); six in `rustgate-server` under `mod mqtt_connections_counters` pin the counter lifecycle end-to-end (registration with the default registry via `init()`, `.name()` and help-text assertions against `gather()`-returned `MetricFamily` instances using the new `prometheus::proto::MetricFamily::name()` accessor — **not** the deprecated `.get_name()` — increment-by-one semantics under `.inc()` using `> before` comparisons — **not** the `int_plus_one`-flagged `>= before + 1` form — and fallback-accessor behaviour when `init()` has not been called so a metrics-off-then-on dev loop does not panic). Three existing MQTT integration-test fixtures in `mqtt_integration.rs` gain `on_accept: None, on_reject: None` to keep compilation green. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds` Prometheus gauge for alertable server-cert rotation SLOs** — `rustgate_server::metrics::init()` now registers an `IntGauge` named `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds` that reports `notAfter - now` in whole seconds for the active server leaf certificate (the one served on every mTLS handshake via the shared `CertReloader`). The value is refreshed as a side effect of `check_tls_cert`, which is called on every `/ready` dependency poll: operators who wire their load-balancer readiness probe to rustgate get a near-real-time expiry signal without a separate scraper. The value can go negative — the gauge *does not* clamp at zero — so an alert rule like `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds < 604800` (7 days remaining) mirrors the existing `check_tls_cert` degradation threshold, and a second rule like `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds < 0` pages on an already-expired cert that is still being served. Alerting only on the readiness endpoint's `degraded`/`unhealthy` verdict would miss the case where an operator has disabled the readiness probe or where the probe target does not scrape the JSON into an alerting backend. Three unit tests pin the contract: `cert_expiry_gauge_set_to_positive_remaining_seconds_for_valid_cert` issues a fresh test cert, calls `check_tls_cert`, and asserts the gauge reads positive; `cert_expiry_gauge_negative_after_expired_cert_check` uses `TestCa::issue_expired_client_cert` to drive the gauge negative and pin the "no clamp at zero" property that alert rules depend on; `cert_expiry_gauge_registered_under_documented_name` walks `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` to confirm the metric is reachable under its stable documented name (a rename would silently break every deployed alert rule). No runtime cost on the handshake hot path — the gauge is only touched during readiness evaluation. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_ws_connections_total` and `rustgate_ws_connections_rejected_total` Prometheus counters** — the WebSocket listener previously emitted no lifecycle telemetry, so a per-CN tenant-quota breach, a connection-rate-limiter trip, an origin/subprotocol rejection, or a global admission-control rejection disappeared silently while the only observable signal was the absence of a subsequent MQTT/CoT message. The new counters close the gap: `rustgate_ws_connections_total` increments once per fully-established WebSocket session (after the HTTP Upgrade succeeds, the per-IP rate limit passes, any Origin/Subprotocol/handshake-limit checks pass, the `WsListener::accept` semaphore admits, TLS completes if enabled, and the router-level `SessionHandle` registers successfully), and `rustgate_ws_connections_rejected_total` increments on every pre-session early-return branch across the two refusal paths — the accept-loop rejections in `WsListener::accept` (per-IP rate limit, semaphore try-acquire, TLS handshake failure, HTTP upgrade failure, origin/subprotocol reject) and the post-accept rejections in `handle_ws_session` (global-admission `BackpressureGate::try_acquire` fail, per-tenant-quota `register` fail). A new `WsSessionHooks { on_connection_accept, on_connection_rejected }` bundle carries the two `Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` callbacks through `WsServerConfig` rather than adding two more bare fields — keeps the `handle_ws_session` signature bounded and matches the `On*` callback idiom already used across the workspace. `WsListenerConfig` gets its own `on_connection_rejected` slot so accept-loop refusals that never reach `handle_ws_session` (the five early returns in `accept()`) still increment; `setup.rs` clones the same `Arc` into both configs under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` so the two refusal paths feed one counter. The transport crate stays metrics-agnostic — no `prometheus` dependency leaks into `rustgate-websocket`; the counters are registered in `rustgate-server::metrics` behind `#[cfg(all(feature = "metrics", feature = "websocket"))]` with the standard `OnceLock + init + accessor + fallback` pattern. Twelve new tests pin the contract: five in `rustgate-websocket::tests::session_hooks` (type-alias `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds, `Debug` redacts closure bodies, `Clone` shares callback state, struct default is all-None, `WsListenerConfig::default` has no reject hook), and seven in `rustgate-server::metrics::tests::ws_connection_counters` (counter registered under documented name × 2, increment observable × 2, full end-to-end callback plumbing × 2, session-hooks struct accepts both counter callbacks × 1). Firing sites explicitly verified: `lib.rs::accept` fires reject on the five early-return branches (rate-limit exceed, semaphore `try_acquire_owned` fail, TLS handshake error, HTTP upgrade error, origin/subprotocol reject); `server.rs::run` fires reject when connection-limit `try_acquire_owned` fails inline; `server.rs::handle_ws_session` fires reject when `BackpressureGate::try_acquire` returns `None` and when `router.register(...)` returns `false` (tenant quota exceeded), and fires accept on the success path immediately after `router.register` returns `true`. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_tls_handshake_duration_seconds` histogram — TLS handshake
  latency by outcome** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/listener.rs` gains
  a new callback slot `OnHandshakeDuration = Arc<dyn Fn(Duration, &str) +
  Send + Sync>` wired through `TlsListener::set_on_handshake_duration`,
  alongside the existing `set_on_handshake_error` hook. The accept loop
  captures `Instant::now()` immediately before the
  `TlsAcceptor::accept(tcp_stream)` future and, on resolution, invokes
  the callback with `handshake_start.elapsed()` plus the outcome label
  (`"success"` when the TLS stream is produced, `"failure"` when the
  acceptor errors). The measurement boundary is deliberately the TLS
  layer itself: it excludes the upstream TCP accept and the downstream
  identity-extraction work so that operators can distinguish
  cryptographic-cost regressions (ring/aws-lc-rs / PQ key-exchange
  changes) from filesystem / CA-chain parsing latency. The observation
  fires on both branches so a handshake that fails fast still contributes
  a data point — without it, dashboards built on
  `histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(rustgate_tls_handshake_duration_seconds_bucket[5m]))`
  would under-count pathology. In `rustgate-server::metrics.rs` the new
  `tls_handshake_duration_seconds()` accessor exposes a registered
  `HistogramVec` with label `outcome` and a tactical bucket range
  `[0.001, 0.005, 0.01, 0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0, 10.0]`
  — the 1 ms floor captures ring's best-case LAN handshakes (~2–5 ms)
  without collapsing the P50 into the first bucket; the 100–500 ms
  brackets cover cross-WAN paths and PQ/hybrid kx groups (ML-KEM-768
  adds ~30–60 ms per round trip on commodity CPUs); the 10 s ceiling
  catches pathological stalls before the TLS acceptor surrenders.
  Registration lives in `init()` and a new `fallback_histogram_vec`
  helper keeps the existing `Histogram`/`HistogramVec` fallback pattern
  symmetric with `fallback_counter_vec` so accessors stay safe when
  `init()` has not been called. In `rustgate-server::setup.rs` an
  additional `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` block installs the observer
  closure immediately after the handshake-error callback, mirroring the
  existing wiring style. Five new tests: `listener.rs` covers the
  setter's overwrite semantics (`set_on_handshake_duration_accepts_and_overwrites_callback`)
  and a compile-time `Send + Sync + 'static` bound on the type alias;
  `metrics.rs` covers canonical-name registration
  (`tls_handshake_duration_is_registered_under_canonical_name`), sum
  delta on observe (`tls_handshake_duration_success_label_observes`),
  label isolation between success / failure series
  (`tls_handshake_duration_failure_label_observes_separately_from_success`),
  and bucket coverage across the floor / ceiling
  (`tls_handshake_duration_bucket_floor_separates_from_ceiling`). Refs #908.
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-server::setup` CLI→subsystem helpers** — `crates/rustgate-server/src/setup.rs` wires every subsystem the server starts (listener, authz, federation, enrollment, NATS, HTTP, UDP, MQTT, WebSocket) but two small pure helpers that every one of those paths depends on had no inline coverage: `validate_framing(&Cli)` (the single gate that decides whether the CLI combination of `--framing` / `--encoding` can start the server at all) and `build_throttle_config(&Cli)` (the mapping from the `--client-message-rate` / `--client-burst` flag pair to the `Option<ThrottleConfig>` the session loop reads on every message). A regression in either silently corrupts every downstream subsystem the same way, and the framing validator has a delicate feature-gate split (`tak-protobuf` framing and encoding are only valid when `proto` is compiled in) that has historically been easy to break. Ten new tests behind a shared `parse_cli(&[&str])` helper that feeds `Cli::try_parse_from(…)` with the required `--cert/--key/--ca` triplet cover the full observable contract. For `validate_framing`: the defaults path (`xml` framing + `raw` encoding) validates; the two classical pairs (`length-prefixed + cot` and `line + raw`) validate; under `#[cfg(feature = "proto")]` the `tak-protobuf + tak-protobuf` pair and the `tak-negotiate + tak-protobuf` pair both validate, while `xml + tak-protobuf` and `length-prefixed + tak-protobuf` are rejected with the error string containing `"--encoding tak-protobuf requires --framing"` so operators can grep the refusal; under `#[cfg(not(feature = "proto"))]` the three protobuf selections (`--framing tak-protobuf`, `--framing tak-negotiate`, `--encoding tak-protobuf`) all surface a startup error whose message contains `"--features proto"` so the operator is pointed at the build-feature fix rather than a generic "invalid config". For `build_throttle_config`: the default `--client-message-rate 0` returns `None` (with an explicit precondition check on `cli.client_message_rate` so a future CLI default change surfaces as a test failure rather than silently turning throttling on for every deployment); an explicit `--client-message-rate 250 --client-burst 500` round-trips both fields into the returned `ThrottleConfig`; and setting only `--client-message-rate 100` threads the documented `--client-burst` default of `200` into the config so a misconfiguration cannot produce a zero-burst throttle that would drop every message. No behavioural change — all added code is `#[cfg(test)]`. (Refs #909)
- **Active WebSocket Ping/Pong health-check** — `crates/rustgate-websocket/src/health.rs` introduces `HealthCheckConfig { ping_interval, pong_timeout }` plus a shared `LastPong = Arc<parking_lot::Mutex<Instant>>` that the read and write halves of a session use to coordinate liveness. `tokio-tungstenite` auto-replies to inbound Pings but never probes the peer, so a session wedged behind a silent NAT/proxy middlebox appears healthy at the library level while no bytes move in either direction. The write loop now selects on a third arm, `tick_or_never(ping_ticker.as_mut())`, which uses `std::future::pending::<Instant>()` when the feature is disabled so the arm stays dormant without allocating a real interval; when enabled it runs a `tokio::time::interval` with `MissedTickBehavior::Delay` at the configured cadence. Each tick snapshots `last_pong` and consults `HealthCheckConfig::is_stale` (which compares `now - last_pong` against `ping_interval + pong_timeout`, using `saturating_duration_since` for clock-skew tolerance); if the peer has gone silent past the deadline the session returns `Err("WebSocket heartbeat timeout: no Pong received")` and the existing unregister/cleanup path tears down the mpsc channels, session handle, and permit. Otherwise the loop emits `Message::Ping(Bytes::new())` under the shared `WRITE_TIMEOUT`. The read loop gains a `Message::Pong(_) =>` arm that takes the `parking_lot::Mutex` lock just long enough to write the current `Instant` — the lock is never held across an `.await`, so the session never risks a deadlock with the write loop's own send. Two new CLI flags plumb through: `--ws-ping-interval-secs` (`RUSTGATE_WS_PING_INTERVAL_SECS`) and `--ws-pong-timeout-secs` (`RUSTGATE_WS_PONG_TIMEOUT_SECS`); both default to `0`, which resolves to `HealthCheckConfig::DISABLED` so existing deployments see zero behaviour change until an operator opts in. A non-zero ping interval with a zero pong timeout keeps Pings flowing forever without terminating the session — useful for warming middleboxes where transient Pong loss must be tolerated. The module ships with seven unit tests covering the disabled constant, the ping-only (zero pong-timeout) case, the in-window and past-deadline staleness checks, the clock-skew path (future `last_pong` values are clamped to zero, not panicked), `new_last_pong` freshness, and `Default == DISABLED`. Closes #904.
- **Release-binary size regression gate** — a new `binary-size` job in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` builds the server with the same feature set shipped by `release-binary.yml` (`nats,enrollment,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,otel,http`), strips it, and compares its byte count against the baseline recorded in `ci/binary-size-baseline.json`. The gate ships with `baseline_bytes = 0`, which makes the first CI run report-only — the job writes a GitHub step-summary table plus a `::notice::` with the observed size and instructs the reviewer to commit that number in a follow-up PR. Once the baseline is set, any PR whose stripped binary exceeds it by more than `_threshold_percent` (default 10 %) fails CI with an `::error::` annotation and a summary row showing baseline / current / delta; the PR either reduces the growth or bumps `baseline_bytes` in the same commit so the delta is visible in code review. Shrinkage passes with a `::notice::` reminding the author to tighten the baseline so future regressions are measured against the new floor. The checker lives in `scripts/check-binary-size.py`: it takes a `<binary-id> <binary-path>` pair, looks the id up under `binaries` in the baseline JSON so multiple binaries (e.g. FIPS, PQ variants) can be tracked independently in future PRs, and emits the same human-readable output to stdout that a developer gets running the script locally against a `cargo build --release` artefact. The baseline file carries an embedded `_comment` pointing future maintainers at the script, and the baseline schema uses a `binaries.<id>.{features,baseline_bytes}` layout so the feature string and the recorded size move together in one reviewable place. (#832)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-transport` TLS listener** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/listener.rs` owns every server-side wiring site's first line of defence — the async `bind` / `bind_with_handshake_limit` entry points, the `DEFAULT_MAX_HANDSHAKES = 100` constant operators size tactical deployments against, the `local_addr()` discovery method every integration test depends on, the `set_on_handshake_error` callback slot the metrics layer wires into, and the custom `Debug` impl that `tracing::info!(?listener, …)` and `tracing::debug!(?listener, …)` render on startup and inside the accept loop — but had zero inline tests; integration-test coverage (`tls_listener.rs`, `crl.rs`, `tls_security.rs`, `pq_kex.rs`) all drive the accept loop end-to-end and so conflate listener construction with handshake and identity extraction. Issue #909 flagged the file as a critical-path gap because a regression in the listener's Debug impl would silently leak the `TlsAcceptor` (and with it the server-cert handle) into operator logs, and a regression in the `DEFAULT_MAX_HANDSHAKES` default would silently change every production deployment's DoS posture. The new `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` adds 14 tests behind a shared `test_tls_config()` helper that installs the rustls default crypto provider idempotently and builds a `TlsConfig` from a fresh `TestCa` — no handshake is ever completed, so the tests stay fast (under 20 ms total) and do not duplicate integration-test territory. Pinned invariants: (a) `DEFAULT_MAX_HANDSHAKES` is exactly `100` — a silent change shows up as a test diff instead of a production capacity surprise; (b) `bind` succeeds on `127.0.0.1:0` and `local_addr()` reports a non-zero ephemeral port on a loopback IP (the two surfaces every test harness depends on); (c) `bind` wires the default limit through to the semaphore — the `max_concurrent_handshakes` field in the Debug output equals `DEFAULT_MAX_HANDSHAKES`, proving the default path has not been rerouted to a magic number; (d) `bind_with_handshake_limit` honours a custom value (tested at both `42` and the boundary `1` for single-permit serialization use-cases); (e) two listeners bound to `127.0.0.1:0` coexist on distinct ephemeral ports so the per-listener accept loop is not accidentally sharing a socket; (f) the `map_err` wrap on the underlying `TcpListener::bind` failure surfaces as a `TransportError::Other` carrying the `"failed to bind TLS listener"` context prefix — exercised by holding a `std::net::TcpListener` on the target port and confirming the second bind fails with that prefix in the error string, so operators keep their triage signal on EADDRINUSE and similar kernel-side bind failures; (g) the Debug impl renders the `TlsListener` type name, embeds the concrete bound port value, keeps the `max_concurrent_handshakes` field visible, and — critically for secret hygiene — renders the `tls_acceptor` field as the fixed `"<TlsAcceptor>"` sentinel rather than letting `TlsAcceptor`'s own Debug leak the server's `ServerConfig` (and thus the server-cert handle) into logs; (h) `set_on_handshake_error` accepts an `OnHandshakeError` callback and overwrites cleanly on a second install (Option-based storage, not append-vec — a future refactor that silently doubled callback fire would fail the second-install case); and (i) a compile-time assertion that `TlsListener: Debug` survives so every `tracing::info!(?listener, …)` site keeps producing useful output at the type level. No behavioural change — all changes are additive test code. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::message` routing contracts** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/message.rs` carries the envelope types (`RoutableMessage`, `Metadata`, `Destination`, `MessageType`) that every inbound message is normalised into before the router sees it, plus the `SOURCE_FEDERATION` and `SOURCE_MAVLINK` wire tags downstream audit/echo-prevention filters key off. The pre-existing inline test module covered only three cases (`MessageType` Display, `Destination` Display for Group/Broadcast/Direct, and the `SOURCE_MAVLINK` constant), leaving four documented contracts pinned nowhere: `Metadata::is_expired`'s clock-skew tolerance, `Destination::kind_str`'s Prometheus-label stability, `Destination::Display` for the `Multicast` variant, and the `SOURCE_FEDERATION` constant. Eleven new tests close the gap. `metadata_is_expired_none_ttl_never_expires`, `metadata_is_expired_past_ttl_is_true`, and `metadata_is_expired_within_ttl_is_false` pin the three ordinary TTL branches — the doc comment explicitly promises that a `None` TTL never expires (federated control traffic relies on this to avoid silently falling out of the position cache) and a regression that flipped the default would be silent otherwise. `metadata_is_expired_future_received_at_treated_as_fresh` pins the clock-skew branch: when `SystemTime::now().elapsed_since(received_at)` returns `Err` because `received_at` is in the future (another node's clock ran ahead), the contract is to treat the message as fresh rather than drop it; a well-intentioned "strictness" refactor that changed the `Err(_) => false` arm to `Err(_) => true` would immediately break federated delivery in any deployment with >0 ms of clock drift. `destination_kind_str_is_low_cardinality_label` pins all four variant strings (`"group"`, `"direct"`, `"broadcast"`, `"multicast"`) — these are both Prometheus label values (where high cardinality is a metrics-backend outage) and SIEM grep tokens (where a rename silently breaks every deployed alerting rule), and the doc comment explicitly calls them "stable across releases". `destination_kind_str_returns_static_str` pins the `&'static str` return type via a compile-time type ascription so a future refactor to an allocating path (e.g. `String`) that would force an allocation on every routed message cannot land silently. `destination_display_multicast_joins_groups_with_commas` and `destination_display_multicast_empty_list` pin the Multicast variant of the Display impl, which the pre-existing tests missed entirely — operators grep for `multicast:[group-name]` substrings in audit output, so the bracket + comma-separated format is a load-bearing contract. `source_federation_constant_is_stable` pins the `"federation"` string value parallel to the existing `SOURCE_MAVLINK` test — this constant is documented in CLAUDE.md's shared-infrastructure table as the authoritative tag for federated messages. `message_type_display_other_preserves_payload` pins the `Other(String)` edge cases (empty inner, punctuation-rich inner) against accidental sanitisation. `message_type_equality_includes_inner_string` pins the derived `PartialEq` semantics on the `Other(String)` variant so a routing path that keys off `MessageType` equality cannot silently collapse two distinct `Other` types. Refs #909.
- **Gap-coverage tests for `rustgate-core::b64` encode/decode helpers** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/b64.rs` is the workspace's single entrypoint for base64 (17+ downstream callers per #830) and backs two cryptographically-sensitive call sites: HMAC digest exchange on the HTTP publisher and JWT header/payload encoding on the federation path. The pre-existing four tests covered the sunny-day round-trips and the §4-alphabet-rejects-§5 direction of engine separation, leaving six observable contracts unpinned where a refactor could silently corrupt callers. Nine new tests close the gaps: `empty_input_round_trips_on_standard_engine` and `empty_input_round_trips_on_url_safe_engine` pin that a zero-length input encodes to an empty string and decodes to an empty `Vec<u8>` — silent emission of stray bytes on empty input would contaminate HMAC digests whose payload is a legitimate empty blob and fabricate JWT parts that look syntactically valid. `decode_rejects_non_alphabet_bytes_with_decode_error` binds the returned error to `DecodeError` to prove the type re-export remains part of the module's public surface — callers in `enrollment/auth.rs` and `federation/jwt.rs` propagate this type through their own error enums with `?`, and a future change that hid the re-export would force every downstream crate to add `base64` as a direct dependency. `decode_url_safe_rejects_standard_padded_output` mirrors the existing `standard_rejects_url_safe_alphabet` test in the reverse direction — `URL_SAFE_NO_PAD` must refuse a canonically-padded blob — so the engine pair is strictly mirrored and a caller cannot accidentally feed a JSON-shaped blob into the JWT decoder and have it silently succeed. `url_safe_rejects_plus_and_slash_alphabet` exercises bytes that produce `+`/`/` under §4 and asserts `URL_SAFE_NO_PAD` rejects them, defending against an engine swap that would silently decode the wrong alphabet. `encode_accepts_all_as_ref_byteslike_types` pins the generic `T: AsRef<[u8]>` bound across every call shape actually used across the 17 downstream crates (slice, array, `&Vec<u8>`, `&String`, `&str`), so a refactor narrowing the bound to `&[u8]` would break callers before CI rather than after release. `large_binary_round_trips_across_chunk_boundaries` round-trips 3 KiB of deterministic binary through both engines — exercising many §4 3-byte-group boundaries past the typical stack-buffer threshold — so a chunking regression surfaces here even when the tiny happy-path round-trips still pass. `standard_engine_produces_padded_output_with_equals` and `url_safe_engine_never_emits_padding` pin the padding contracts at the three length-mod-3 boundaries (1-byte → `==`, 2-byte → `=`, 3-byte → none; URL-safe → never), so snapshot tests and wire fixtures that rely on the exact string shape continue to match after an engine-config change. All tests operate on in-memory buffers — no network, no filesystem, no feature gating — and add zero runtime to the test binary (the existing `rustgate-core` library suite still runs 150 tests in ~1.7s). Refs #909.
- **Gap-coverage tests for `rustgate-client::reconnect` backoff, jitter, and fatal-error classification** — `crates/rustgate-client/src/reconnect.rs` is the resilience heart of every long-lived client in the workspace, but its prior single test covered only the exponential-growth happy-path of `compute_backoff`. Ten new tests pin the remaining observable contracts that resilience depends on. `default_policy_matches_documented_values` pins `ReconnectPolicy::default()` at 100 ms / 30 s / 10 attempts, so a change to any of these is a reviewable diff rather than a silent behavioural shift that every downstream `ClientConfig::reconnect(Default::default())` caller inherits. `backoff_attempt_zero_uses_base_interval_without_shift` guards the `saturating_sub(1)` shift path — a refactor that replaced the saturating sub with `-` would underflow `u32` and crash on the unusual (but legal) `attempt == 0` call site; the sampled distribution must stay within the base ±20 % band. `backoff_clamps_shift_at_attempt_twenty_one` fires `attempt ∈ {21, 100, 1_000, u32::MAX}` through the function to pin the `.min(20)` shift cap — without the cap, `1u64 << 64` is undefined behaviour, but with the cap every over-sized attempt still lands inside the `max_backoff` ceiling with jitter. `backoff_with_zero_initial_returns_zero` and `backoff_with_zero_max_returns_zero` pin the two nonsensical-but-legal zero-policy cases where the jitter helper must short-circuit rather than call `fastrand::u64(0..=0)` — a future `fastrand` bump that rejects empty-range sampling would otherwise panic in production. `backoff_jitter_actually_varies_within_band` samples 64 draws at attempt 5 and asserts the spread exceeds 100 ms; without jitter, multiple clients that all fail at the same instant re-dial in lockstep (the thundering-herd pathology the whole primitive exists to prevent), so a regression to constant output fails CI. `is_fatal_dial_error_returns_true_for_tls_cert_and_missing_field` pins the three variants (`Tls`, `CertParse`, `MissingField`) that cause the loop to give up immediately — retrying a TLS config error cannot succeed without operator intervention, so treating it as transient would produce a log-storm spinning through the full retry budget. `is_fatal_dial_error_returns_false_for_transient_and_io_failures` covers the matching converse across `Io(ConnectionRefused)`, `Io(NetworkUnreachable)`, `ConnectTimeout`, `Disconnected`, `ReconnectExhausted`, and `Lagged`; the last three are not produced by the dial path today but are included so a classification refactor that silently widened the fatal arm still fails. `reconnect_policy_is_copy_and_clone` pins both traits with a move-then-use pattern that only compiles while `Copy` is implemented, so dropping `Copy` (and forcing `.clone()` on every reconnect-loop iteration) is caught at test time rather than via a silent perf regression across every client. `reconnect_policy_debug_exposes_all_fields` asserts the three field names and the `max_attempts` value appear in the `#[derive(Debug)]` output — operators grep production logs for this repr on misbehaving clients, so a hand-rolled `impl Debug` that omits a field would render them blind. All tests are self-contained (no tokio runtime, no sleeps, no sockets); the added 10 tests bring the module from 1 test to 11. Refs #909.

- **Per-active-segment size trigger for the CoT archive rotation task** — `rustgate_server::archive_rotation::RotationConfig` gains a `max_active_bytes: u64` knob wired through the new `--archive-max-active-bytes` CLI flag / `RUSTGATE_ARCHIVE_MAX_ACTIVE_BYTES` env var / `archive-max-active-bytes` TOML key (default `0` = unlimited). The pre-existing `archive_rotation.rs` operated in three modes — age-based sweep (`max_age`), directory-total size sweep (`max_bytes`), and the implicit daily boundary inside `CotArchiver::write_line` keyed on UTC date — which between them guaranteed the active segment could not exceed 24 hours of growth, but put no bound on how large a single segment could become inside that window. A misbehaving authz policy that emits an audit record per message (the compound risk called out in `#887` Part 4.4 — `Obligation::Unknown("evil")` at per-message rate) or an abusive client running a hot loop against the publish path could push a single daily file into the tens-of-gigabytes range, degrading startup tail-recovery (F-32 rewinds through the last 16 MiB looking for the chain tip, but the file can be arbitrarily larger than that) and filling disk before the next time-based rotation tick lands. The new knob closes the compound half of #887 Part 4.4: on each rotation tick, before the directory sweep, the background task calls `CotArchiver::roll_if_over_size(max_active_bytes)` — which flushes the current writer, measures the on-disk size via `std::fs::metadata`, and if the file exceeds the cap, drops the writer (sealing encrypted files via `EncryptedWriter::Drop`), renames the closed file to carry a `T<hh-mm-ss>` stamp before its extension, and clears the archiver's `current_date` so the next `record()` call re-opens a fresh segment at the stable `cot-<date>.jsonl[.enc]` path. The rolled file is then immediately eligible for eviction under `max_bytes` / `max_age` in the same pass, so operators who set all three knobs get a coherent "cap per-file, cap total, cap age" policy. Seven new inline tests in `archive.rs` pin every state: `rolled_path_inserts_stamp_before_jsonl_plaintext` / `rolled_path_inserts_stamp_before_jsonl_encrypted` codify the suffix-placement contract (plaintext and encrypted paths share identical stamp placement, encrypted files keep the `.enc` trailing suffix); `roll_if_over_size_rolls_plaintext_segment` exercises the full roll cycle and asserts the next `record()` opens a fresh stable-path segment after the rename; `roll_if_over_size_rolls_encrypted_segment_and_round_trips` is the correctness-critical test — it writes through the `EncryptedWriter`, rolls mid-stream, and round-trips the rolled `.enc` file through `rustgate_core::encrypted::decrypt_file`; without the writer-drop sealing, this test would fail with `MissingTerminator`, so it pins the requirement that rolling plays correctly with ChaCha20-Poly1305 stream termination; `roll_if_over_size_no_op_when_writer_not_open` handles the pre-first-write edge case; `roll_if_over_size_no_op_when_under_cap` and `roll_if_over_size_zero_cap_is_disabled` pin the feature-off postures. Additionally, `active_path()` is fixed to include the `.enc` suffix when encryption is configured — a latent bug that made the rotation sweep's active-file exclusion silently ineffective on encrypted archives, so an ancient-but-active `.enc` segment could be deleted by the age or directory-size sweep while the writer still had it open. `active_path_encrypted_carries_enc_suffix` pins the fix so it cannot regress. (#887 Part 4.4)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-http` SSRF-safe DNS resolver** — `crates/rustgate-http/src/resolver.rs` is the runtime half of the SSRF defence-in-depth pair: `config.rs` validates the URL at load time (covered extensively by 35+ inline tests), and `SsrfSafeResolver` re-checks the resolved IP addresses at connection time to close the DNS-rebinding TOCTOU gap. An attacker who controls a hostname whose `A`/`AAAA` record initially pointed at a public IP (passing config validation) but flips to `127.0.0.1` or `169.254.169.254` by the time the first HTTP request fires would previously have slipped through unnoticed; only the runtime filter in `resolver.rs` catches that flip, and prior to this PR that file had zero coverage. Seven new inline tests pin the full observable contract: `new_preserves_allow_private_true` and `new_preserves_allow_private_false` pin that the constructor stores the flag both ways (a regression here silently neutralises either the test-fixture escape hatch or the production filter); `resolve_rejects_localhost_by_default` exercises the production path end-to-end — `/etc/hosts` resolves `localhost` to `127.0.0.1`, the resolver filters it, and the returned error carries the three operator-grep-grade tokens `"SSRF protection"`, `"private/reserved"`, and `"localhost"`; `resolve_rejection_surfaces_as_permission_denied` downcasts the boxed error to `std::io::Error` and asserts `ErrorKind::PermissionDenied` so a future refactor cannot silently swap it for `InvalidData` and change retry/circuit-breaker classification; `resolve_allows_localhost_when_escape_hatch_set` pins that `allow_private = true` lets the integration-test wiring reach `127.0.0.1` and every yielded address is actually loopback; `resolve_propagates_dns_failure_without_wrapping` uses the RFC 6761 §6.4-reserved `.invalid` TLD — guaranteed to never resolve on any host — to assert that a pure DNS failure does not get relabelled as an SSRF rejection, so callers can distinguish "name does not exist" from "name resolves to a blocked IP"; `resolve_escape_hatch_does_not_filter_public_hostnames` uses `.invalid` under `allow_private = true` to pin that the escape hatch is a pure pass-through, not a loopback-only special case, and must never inject its own rejection on the allow path. All tests use `reqwest::dns::Name::from_str` for input construction (the only public constructor) and `tokio::net::lookup_host` for DNS resolution; no network egress required — `localhost` resolves via `/etc/hosts` and `.invalid` fails at the resolver stub. Refs #909.

### Fixed

- **Refresh TLS `root_hint_subjects` on CA rotation and surface BackpressureGate setter misordering (F-50, F-51)** — two correctness fixes in `rustgate-transport` that were both silent-failure bugs: wrong output with no log line. **F-50** — `DynamicCertVerifier::root_hint_subjects` previously snapshotted the initial CA list at construction and never updated it. After a SIGHUP-triggered trust-store reload added a new CA, the live verifier would correctly validate chains under that CA, but the TLS `CertificateRequest` handshake message kept advertising the stale list, so a client whose issuing CA was only added post-startup would see its CA missing from `certificate_authorities` and some mTLS stacks (notably the RFC-strict ones and older rustls clients) would decline to present a certificate — the operator would observe "cert is loaded but the client still can't authenticate" with no handshake-layer error pointing at the hint staleness. The fix replaces the `Vec<DistinguishedName>` field with `ArcSwap<&'static [DistinguishedName]>` and rebuilds the slice alongside the inner `WebPkiClientVerifier` inside `current_verifier()` on every trust-store generation advance. Escaping the `arc_swap::Guard` lifetime to return a `&[DN]` with `&self` lifetime (required by the `ClientCertVerifier` trait) without `unsafe_code` is resolved by leaking each refreshed slice with `Vec::leak` — the `'static` payload trivially outlives any `&self` borrow, and the leak is bounded by the rotation rate (rare; a few hundred bytes per CA rotation). On rebuild failure the fresh slice is *not* leaked — the old hints stay in lockstep with the old cached verifier so handshakes never advertise CAs whose keys we cannot validate against. **F-51** — `BackpressureGate::with_resume_dwell`, `set_on_deferred`, and `set_on_bytes_changed` all rely on `Arc::get_mut`, which only succeeds while the `Arc` has a single strong reference. Before this fix the setters used `debug_assert!` + `if let Some(this) = Arc::get_mut(self)` + silent no-op; in release builds `debug_assert!` is stripped, so calling any of the three setters after the gate had already been cloned into the router and the accept loop would drop the configuration on the floor with no log, no panic, and no error. The most visible symptom was `admission_deferred_total` / `queued_bytes` gauges frozen at zero despite the gate actively throttling — an operator reading the dashboard would see "backpressure is healthy" while the system was in fact refusing new connections. All three setters now return `Result<(), BackpressureConfigError::AlreadyShared { setter }>` naming the offending setter, and `rustgate-server::main::run` propagates the error via `anyhow::Context` with a human-readable wrapper so startup fails loudly instead of limping along with missing instrumentation. Five new regression tests pin the contracts: `root_hint_subjects_refreshes_after_ca_rotation` confirms a second CA surfaces in the hint list after a generation bump; `root_hint_subjects_keeps_old_list_on_rebuild_error` confirms a failed rebuild leaves the previous hint list intact (not leaked, not partially replaced); `setters_return_err_after_clone` exercises each of the three setters against an already-cloned `Arc` and asserts the error variant; `setters_succeed_before_clone` confirms the happy path is unchanged; `config_error_display_names_setter` pins the `Display` output contains the setter name so an operator's log grep can identify the misordered call site. (Refs #814)
- **Backpressure gate no longer oscillates at the high-water boundary; hot-path callbacks must be non-blocking** — `rustgate_transport::BackpressureGate` now requires the in-flight byte counter to stay at or below `low_water` continuously for at least `resume_dwell` (default **1 second**) before admission resumes after a block. Previously, any drop to `low_water - 1` immediately cleared `blocking` and woke the accept loop, so a workload that hovered between `low_water + 1` and `low_water - 1` (e.g. one slow client feeding an evenly-sized fan-out) would flip admission on and off at the cadence of the TCP handshake loop — each new session's first message pushed the counter back over `high_water`, the permit drop dipped it under `low_water`, and the thrashing wasted CPU, spammed the `admission_deferred_total` metric, and could starve the server's per-host handshake-parallelism budget. The fix is an atomic `AtomicU64` cooldown timestamp (`below_low_since`, relative to a per-gate `tokio::time::Instant` epoch so `start_paused` tests stay deterministic under the virtual clock) that (a) arms on the first `sub` that drops the counter to or below `low_water` while blocking, (b) clears on any subsequent `add` that pushes back above `low_water`, and (c) is read by both `sub` and a new 100ms poll branch inside `wait_for_capacity` so dwell expiry can release the accept loop even when no further `sub` arrives. The hot path remains lock-free: `sub` short-circuits the dwell bookkeeping entirely when the gate is not blocking. A new `--backpressure-resume-dwell-ms` CLI flag (env `RUSTGATE_BACKPRESSURE_RESUME_DWELL_MS`, default 1000) lets operators tune the dwell — setting `0` restores pre-fix behaviour for bursty-but-not-oscillatory workloads where the latency cost matters. Separately, the `OnDeferred` and `OnBytesChanged` callback type docs now explicitly require non-blocking bodies: the gauge/counter update path fires on every byte-count change (i.e. potentially once per message), and `wait_for_capacity` is awaited on the TLS accept loop — a blocking callback there stalls the entire accept path and breaks admission control. Six new regression tests using `#[tokio::test(start_paused = true)]` + `tokio::time::advance` cover: default dwell keeps the gate blocked until the timer elapses, oscillation at the boundary cancels the in-flight cooldown, `wait_for_capacity` wakes via the internal poll without any external `sub`, a re-cross above `high_water` resets the dwell window, explicit `Duration::ZERO` preserves the legacy immediate-resume behaviour byte-for-byte, and the `Debug` impl surfaces the new field so operator log greps see it. (#878)
- **Gate `TestNatsServer::start()` on JetStream readiness, not just TCP `INFO` greeting** — `rustgate-test-utils`'s `TestNatsServer::try_start` previously returned as soon as the spawned `nats-server` subprocess replied to the NATS protocol `INFO` greeting, but `nats-server --jetstream` binds its TCP listener well before the JetStream subsystem finishes initialising and the meta-leader is elected. On a loaded runner the gap between "TCP accepts connections" and "JS API accepts requests" exceeds the 5s per-operation JetStream timeout, so any test that calls `jetstream.create_key_value(…)` (or `create_stream`, `create_consumer`, …) on the context returned from `TestNatsServer::jetstream()` flakes with `JetStream error: timed out` on the very first call. The symptom observed in CI was `create cm_b: failed to create federation-claims KV bucket` in `rustgate-federation::ha_integration::claim_gates_peer_connection`, but every one of the 14 `ha_integration.rs` tests and every current or future NATS-backed test in the workspace is exposed to the same race. `try_start` now chains a second readiness probe after the `INFO` greeting succeeds: a throwaway `async_nats::connect` + `jetstream::new` + `query_account` round-trip that maps to `$JS.API.ACCOUNT.INFO`, which only returns `Ok` once the JS meta-leader has been elected. The probe is bounded by the existing `READY_TIMEOUT` deadline so a genuinely broken JetStream configuration still fails fast, and its connection is dropped before `start()` returns so callers always hold a fresh client created through `TestNatsServer::jetstream()`. A new unit test in `test_utils::nats::tests` — `start_completes_only_after_jetstream_accepts_kv_create` — pins the invariant by creating a KV bucket and round-tripping a `put`/`get` immediately after `start()` returns, with no retry loop around the KV call. Also enables the async-nats `kv` feature on `rustgate-test-utils` so the regression test can construct a KV config without dragging in a parallel feature flag. (Fixes the flake that broke #976's CI on 2026-04-19.)
- **Relax PQ kx-group assertions so FIPS-plus-post-quantum CI stops reporting spurious failures** — `crates/rustgate-transport/tests/pq_kex.rs` had two assertions that were correct for the single-feature `post-quantum` build but deterministically failed under the CI command `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-transport --features proto,fips,post-quantum,ocsp,spiffe`: `pq_provider_includes_mlkem_groups` required `X25519` in `aws_lc_rs::default_provider().kx_groups`, and `pq_classical_client_falls_back_to_x25519` required the negotiated group between a ring-backed client and the PQ-backed server to be exactly `X25519`. When `rustls/fips` is active, `default_provider()` is restricted to FIPS-approved groups — `X25519MLKEM768` and the NIST curves `secp256r1/384r1/521r1` — and drops `X25519`, which is not FIPS-approved. The original issue (#957) framed this as a multi-test race against the process-global `CryptoProvider`, but the failure reproduces in isolation and repeats deterministically across runs: there is no race, it is a feature-combination mismatch. The provider-shape test now asserts the invariant we actually care about — "hybrid PQ is offered *and* at least one classical group remains negotiable" — accepting any of `X25519`, `secp256r1`, `secp384r1`, or `secp521r1` as the classical fallback. The negotiation test now asserts the negotiated group is *some* classical (non-PQ) group, because the correctness condition for "graceful fallback from a ring-only client" is "non-PQ group was chosen", not the specific identity of that group; FIPS forces the pair onto `secp256r1` where non-FIPS lands on `X25519`, and both outcomes are correct fallbacks. Verified by running the file under both feature sets: `--features post-quantum` → 8/8 pass, `--features proto,fips,post-quantum,ocsp,spiffe` → 8/8 pass. (#957)

### Security

- **Cap `ConnRateLimiter` IP map under IPv6 prefix scan and strip ANSI CSI sequences in `sanitize_for_log` (F-34, F-35)** — two core hardening fixes in `rustgate-resilience` and `rustgate-core` that close memory-exhaustion and log-injection residue under adversarial input. **F-34** — `rustgate_resilience::conn_rate_limiter::ConnRateLimiter` previously allocated a `VecDeque<Instant>` for every distinct `IpAddr` the MQTT and WebSocket listeners saw, with no hard cap and no IPv6 prefix collapsing. An attacker with a single /48 IPv6 allocation (2^80 source addresses, routinely available on residential ISPs and cloud VPS providers) could cycle source addresses by incrementing the host bits and bloat the per-limiter `HashMap<IpAddr, VecDeque<Instant>>` past server RAM in seconds, OOM-killing the process — the sliding-window sweep only fires lazily when the map grows past the 512-entry threshold, which is orders of magnitude below the attacker's flood rate. The fix is two-layer. A new `canonical_key` function normalizes every incoming address before insertion: IPv4 stays at /32 granularity (address scarcity bounds diversity) while IPv6 collapses to the /64 prefix (the smallest IPv6 allocation under RFC 4291 SLAAC), so an attacker cycling host bits within a /64 now cannot evade the rate limit and cannot inflate the map past one entry per /64. A new hard cap `DEFAULT_MAX_TRACKED_IPS = 65_536` — exactly 2^16, the worst-case number of /64 prefixes within a /48 allocation — bounds the map regardless of how many /64s the attacker controls; when the cap is reached and a previously-unseen key arrives, the entry with the oldest last-seen timestamp is evicted to make room (O(n) in the cap, but only fires when the cap is actually reached, which under realistic workloads never happens). The cap is configurable via the new `with_max_tracked_ips(usize)` builder method so tests can exercise eviction paths without allocating 64k entries. Six new regression tests pin the contract: `ipv6_addresses_in_same_64_share_bucket` confirms two IPv6 hosts differing only in the lower 64 bits are rate-limited as a single bucket, `ipv6_different_64_prefixes_are_isolated` confirms distinct /64 prefixes retain independent buckets, `ipv4_unaffected_by_prefix_collapse` confirms IPv4 is tracked at /32 (not lumped into /24 or similar), `map_size_capped_under_prefix_scan` walks 4× the cap through distinct /64 prefixes and asserts the map never exceeds the cap, `eviction_drops_least_recently_seen_key` seeds two IPs with ordered timestamps and asserts the older one is evicted when a third unique key arrives, and `repeat_inserts_on_same_key_do_not_trigger_eviction` confirms the cap guard only fires for previously-unseen keys (a legitimate client returning inside the window must not be dropped to make room for itself). **F-35** — `rustgate_core::sanitize::sanitize_for_log` previously replaced each control character (including `\x1b` ESC and `\x9b` C1 CSI) with `U+FFFD` using a per-character map, but the rest of the CSI escape sequence — parameter bytes like `[31m` — consists of printable ASCII and sailed through untouched. A malicious certificate CN like `"alice\x1b[1;31mFAKE LOG ENTRY\x1b[0m"` would appear in a log aggregator as `"alice�[1;31mFAKE LOG ENTRY�[0m"` — the introducer byte was replaced but the SGR parameters and final byte still rendered as styled text on any terminal downstream of the replaced ESC (an attacker can still force the log line to look like a different category of event to a SOC analyst grepping the file). The fix replaces the per-character `.map(...)` with a state-machine pass: on encountering `\x1b` (C0) or `\x9b` (C1), consume the full CSI body — parameter and intermediate bytes in `0x20..=0x3F`, terminated by a final byte in `0x40..=0x7E`, per ECMA-48 §5.4 — and replace the entire sequence with a single `U+FFFD`. Truncated sequences at end-of-input are handled by natural EOF on the inner loop; invalid inner bytes (e.g. a newline inside a CSI body) terminate the CSI scan and sanitize normally. Non-CSI ESC sequences (`\x1b` followed by a non-`[` byte like OSC `\x1b]…\x07` or `\x1bM`) still only replace the introducer byte — the issue scope is strictly CSI, and broader ESC-sequence stripping is deferred to a follow-up. Six new regression tests in `sanitize::tests` pin the contract: `ansi_csi_fully_consumed` covers red-SGR-around-text with multiple parameter groups, `c1_csi_fully_consumed` covers the 8-bit `\u{9B}` introducer and intermediate+final sequences like `\u{9B}?25l`, `esc_without_bracket_only_replaces_introducer` confirms non-CSI ESC sequences are not over-consumed, `truncated_csi_does_not_leak_body` confirms unterminated CSI at end-of-input produces a single U+FFFD with no body leak, `csi_terminated_by_invalid_byte_stops_cleanly` confirms a newline inside a CSI body cleanly terminates the scan and sanitizes the newline, and `log_forging_with_ansi_sequence_cannot_inject_styled_tail` is the end-to-end attacker-scenario regression. The five pre-existing tests (normal strings, newlines, control chars, unicode preservation, empty) continue to pass without modification — the single-character replacement path is preserved for every byte that is not a CSI introducer. (#810)
- **Bump `rustls-webpki` 0.103.12 → 0.103.13 to resolve RUSTSEC-2026-0104 (reachable panic in CRL parsing)** — `rustls-webpki` 0.103.12 contained a reachable panic in certificate-revocation-list parsing that an attacker who could feed a crafted CRL into the verification path could use to crash the process. rustgate consumes `rustls-webpki` transitively through `rustls` (TLS listener + client), `async-nats` (NATS/JetStream TLS client), and the federation/HTTP egress stacks, so every rustgate binary that loads an operator-supplied or peer-supplied CRL was in scope. Patched in 0.103.13; the fix is a pure `cargo update -p rustls-webpki --precise 0.103.13` on `Cargo.lock` and `fuzz/Cargo.lock` — no source changes are required because no crate pins an upper bound below 0.103.13 in `[workspace.dependencies]`. `cargo deny check advisories` now reports `ok` on both main and every open PR that rebased past this commit. (RUSTSEC-2026-0104)
- **Migrate HTTP-egress `pin-sha256` to RFC 7469 SPKI pinning** — `rustgate_http::pinning::PinnedCertVerifier` now computes `SHA-256(SubjectPublicKeyInfo DER)` on the leaf certificate and compares against the configured pins, rather than hashing the entire leaf DER. The old full-DER semantic was out of step with every mainstream pinning tool: Chromium, Mozilla, the RFC 7469 / HPKP `report-uri` ecosystem, and the standard OpenSSL pin recipe (`openssl x509 -pubkey -noout | openssl pkey -pubin -outform der | openssl dgst -sha256 -binary | base64`) all hash SPKI. Operators generating pins from any of those sources would silently fail-closed against a rustgate binary because the pin in their config file matched the public key, not the whole cert. Worse, the previous behaviour actively punished good DevOps hygiene: a same-key cert rotation (fresh serial, fresh validity window, fresh SAN list, but the long-lived key material preserved) mutates the leaf DER and therefore mutated every deployed full-DER pin — every annual cert roll on every edge became a flag-day config push, with the inevitable result that tired operators just widened `pin-sha256` to an empty list and quietly turned off defense-in-depth. SPKI pinning inverts that: the pin survives same-key rotation and only breaks on the security-relevant event of a real key change, which is when an operator *does* want to know. The module-level docs in `crates/rustgate-http/src/pinning.rs`, the `TlsConfig::pin_sha256` field doc in `config.rs`, and the `docs/http-egress.md` Certificate Pinning section all document the RFC 7469 semantic and embed the canonical OpenSSL generation recipe. A new private `extract_spki_der` helper fails closed on a malformed cert via `TlsError::General("certificate SPKI extraction failed: …")` rather than panicking or silently admitting the connection, and is shared between the verifier hot path and a test-only `spki_pin` helper so the pin computation cannot drift between production and test fixtures. New `rustgate_test_utils::certs::generate_server_keypair` plus `TestCa::issue_server_cert_with_key(&KeyPair, cn, sans)` let tests model the same-key-different-cert flow; the existing `issue_server_cert` is refactored to delegate to the new helper so single-call usage is unchanged. Five new regression tests in `pinning::tests` pin the full observable contract: `distinct_keypairs_hash_to_distinct_spki_pins` (independent keys yield independent pins — the core key-sensitivity property that keeps a CA-compromise attacker from swapping the leaf), `spki_pin_survives_same_key_reissue` (the direct #948 regression guard — asserts reissued leaf DERs differ but SPKI bytes and SPKI pins stay identical), `verifier_accepts_matching_spki_pin_from_real_cert` (round-trips a real issued cert's pin through `b64::encode` → `parse_pins` to mirror config-file ingestion and confirm the verifier's hot path and the config loader compute the same 32 bytes), `legacy_full_der_pin_no_longer_matches` (a pre-#948 full-DER pin must not collide with the new SPKI pin for the same cert — silent acceptance would defeat the whole migration), and `extract_spki_der_rejects_malformed_cert` (the helper surfaces an x509-parser error as a `TlsError::General` so the handshake path fails closed on a malformed cert, not on a panic). **Breaking change for operators:** existing full-DER pins from pre-#948 deployments will stop matching on upgrade; regenerate each pin with the OpenSSL recipe in `docs/http-egress.md` or the module docs. A rolling upgrade can overlap both forms by running the old and new pin simultaneously across a canary window, since both are independent 32-byte base64 strings and the pin array is OR-matched. (Closes #948)
- **Guard release-artifact filenames against co-packaging collisions (F-21)** — `release-binary.yml` builds six archives from a 2×3 matrix (x86_64/aarch64 × base/fips/pq) and the "Package binary" shell step names each one `rustgate[-fips|-pq]-${VERSION}-${TARGET}.tar.gz`. The distinct discriminators (`fips`, `pq`, or unmarked) were correct as of today but the workflow had no structural guard against a future matrix edit silently producing two entries that compute the same archive name — `gh release upload --clobber` would then overwrite one with the other, the cosign signature and build-provenance attestation would be bound to whichever variant wrote last, and downstream `cosign verify-blob` consumers would admit a binary under the wrong identity. A new `scripts/check-release-artifact-names.py` parses `.github/workflows/release-binary.yml`, re-implements the Package-binary naming logic against each matrix entry, and asserts three invariants: (1) every entry produces a distinct archive filename, (2) each non-base entry carries its discriminator token (`-fips-` / `-pq-`) in the filename while the base build carries neither, and (3) every filename matches the shape `rustgate-[fips-|pq-]<version>-<target>.tar.gz`. A `fips=true + pq=true` combination is explicitly rejected as a misconfiguration (the workflow's `if/elif` would silently pick `fips`, making the `pq=true` flag a dead marker). The script runs in the existing `playbook-lint` CI job on every PR — not only those touching the workflow — so a rename in an unrelated helper that the workflow references surfaces before a release tag cuts in, and uses the standard `::error file=…::` annotation format so the GitHub UI highlights the offending line. Closes the F-21 caveat from the residual-findings audit (#887). The F-21 primary advisory (aws-lc-sys VEX entry, RUSTSEC-2026-0049) was already resolved in #777 — this PR closes the forward-looking "don't accidentally co-package" half that was deferred to a separate ticket. (#887)
- **Harden encrypted-archive writer against symlink TOCTOU and ChaCha20-Poly1305 nonce reuse** — `rustgate_core::encrypted::EncryptedWriter::create` now opens the target file with `O_NOFOLLOW` on Unix (via `OpenOptionsExt::custom_flags` with a platform-pinned `O_NOFOLLOW` constant), translating the kernel's `ELOOP`/`EMLINK` refusal into a new `EncryptError::SymlinkTarget { path }` variant. On a multi-tenant host an attacker who can plant a symlink at the archive path (store-forward queue, encrypted audit log) could previously redirect our `truncate + write` into any file the service account can open, silently overwriting unrelated data; `O_NOFOLLOW` is the atomic TOCTOU-free defense and intermediate components are still followed, matching the scope of the threat. A regression test plants a sensitive file under a symlink at the target path and asserts both that `create()` returns `SymlinkTarget` and that the sensitive file's contents survive untouched. Separately, the 32-bit per-file chunk counter used for nonce derivation — `nonce = base_nonce (8 bytes) || counter_be (4 bytes)` — had a reuse window at exhaustion: the old `write_chunk` / `finish` path incremented `counter` via `checked_add(1)` *after* sealing and persisting the chunk, so when `counter == u32::MAX` the ciphertext with nonce `(base, u32::MAX)` landed on disk, the increment errored, and `counter` stayed pinned at `u32::MAX`. A caller that ignored the `CounterOverflow` error (or subsequently called `finish()`) would re-seal under the same nonce — catastrophic for ChaCha20-Poly1305. Both methods now reserve the post-increment *before* computing the nonce and performing the AEAD seal, so refusal is atomic: if we cannot advance, we do not write. A new regression test sets the counter to `u32::MAX`, confirms `write_chunk` returns `CounterOverflow` without appending any bytes, confirms the counter is not silently wrapped, confirms a follow-up `finish()` also refuses, and confirms the resulting file — intentionally missing a terminator — is rejected by `decrypt_file` as `Truncated` so operators are forced to rotate the key or file instead of trusting a half-sealed archive. (#809)
- **Persist audit-chain tip across restarts for cross-boot tamper detection** — `rustgate_core::audit_chain` gains an explicit `<audit.jsonl>.anchor` sidecar file that pins the SHA-256 tip of the hash-chained audit log on clean shutdown and lets the next boot prove the chain was not tampered with while the service was down. `HashChainWriter` gains `with_tip_observer(Arc<ArcSwap<String>>)`: the writer atomically publishes its current chain tip on every chain advance, so shutdown code owning the observer can snapshot the latest hash even though the writer itself is hidden inside a `Mutex<Box<dyn Write>>` owned by the `tracing` subscriber. A new `check_chain_continuity(path) -> ChainContinuity` function reads the live tail hash via the existing torn-write-tolerant `read_chain_tip` helper and compares it to the persisted anchor, returning `NoAnchor` (fresh install — benign, no previous shutdown to match), `Match { tip }` (anchor agrees with live tip — clean restart, no tampering), or `Mismatch { anchor_tip, live_tip }` (anchor disagrees — the log was truncated, extended, or rewritten while the service was stopped). The `rustgate-server` binary wires all three pieces end-to-end: on startup it runs the continuity check against the configured `--audit-file` (plain-file path only; the rotating appender path is left for a follow-up), and on `Mismatch` it bails via `anyhow::bail!` so a hostile actor cannot silently edit the log offline and have the next boot continue the chain from the forged tip. Operators needing a one-shot escape hatch — e.g. intentional log rotation outside the service's lifecycle, or a manual archival operation that updates the tail — opt in via the new `--audit-allow-chain-break` flag (env `RUSTGATE_AUDIT_ALLOW_CHAIN_BREAK`, defaults `false`), which downgrades the break from fatal to audited: the new `audit::chain_discontinuity(audit_path, anchor_tip, live_tip, action, reason)` event is emitted after the tracing subscriber comes up, so the break is itself recorded as a cryptographically chained audit record from the new live tip forward. On graceful shutdown, the server snapshots the observed tip, calls `write_anchor`, and emits either `audit::chain_anchor_persisted` or `audit::chain_anchor_persist_failed` depending on whether the sidecar file was written. Ten new unit tests in `audit_chain.rs` cover the anchor write/read round-trip, malformed/truncated anchor payloads, the continuity verdicts (no anchor, match, mismatch against post-anchor extension, mismatch against post-anchor truncation), observer updates on chain advance, and a full end-to-end lifecycle test that models the production path: writer → observer snapshot → anchor write → next-boot continuity check returns `Match`. Two new CLI tests pin the default-false posture of `--audit-allow-chain-break` and its opt-in behaviour. (#718)
- **Tenant-isolated cross-path dedup cache** — `rustgate_transport::DedupCache` now folds the sender's tenant (derived from the client cert's X.509 Organization field, exposed via `Identity.tenant`) into the SHA-256 key preimage alongside the existing `uid || 0x00 || time_le || 0x00 || payload` slots. Previously a message sent by tenant `A` could suppress a byte-identical message sent by tenant `B`, opening a timing side-channel where a malicious tenant could probe for the existence of specific messages in another tenant by submitting candidate `(uid, time, payload)` triples and observing whether delivery succeeded (= the other tenant did not send this) or was silently deduplicated (= the other tenant sent exactly this). The router hot path at `route_inner` now passes `msg.source.tenant.as_deref().unwrap_or("")` into `check_payload`; the fallback to an empty string preserves pre-tenant behaviour for clients whose certificate has no Organization field (they share one tenancy slot by convention). Single-tenant deployments, or multi-tenant deployments where cross-tenant duplicate suppression is explicitly desired, can opt back into the pre-#722 behaviour via the new `--dedup-cross-tenant` CLI flag (env `RUSTGATE_DEDUP_CROSS_TENANT`, default `false`) and the corresponding `dedup-cross-tenant` TOML key. The flag is wired through `DedupCache::with_cross_tenant(bool)` — a stance-setter, fixed at construction time, not a runtime toggle — and the server logs a `WARN` on startup when cross-tenant mode is active so the trade-off is visible in the operational audit trail. Five new regression tests in `dedup.rs` (`different_tenants_same_payload_pass_independently`, `same_tenant_same_payload_still_deduped`, `cross_tenant_mode_suppresses_across_tenants`, `missing_tenant_treated_as_empty`, `tenant_affects_check_payload_too`) pin both the default and the opt-in behaviour. The protocol reference in `docs/protocol.md` §4.3 documents the new key format, the threat model, and the unsafe-opt-in caveat. (#722)
- **Refuse plaintext enrollment and fail-closed on rate-limiter mutex poison** — `rustgate_enrollment::EnrollmentServer::run` now rejects an empty/whitespace `cert_pem` or `key_pem` up front with an explicit `EnrollmentError::Config` that names the refusal — "enrollment server requires TLS … refusing to start". The downstream TLS builder would also reject empty inputs via `parse_certs`/`parse_key`, but its generic "failed to parse server certs" error hides the security rationale: PKCS#12 enrollment responses embed **unencrypted** certificate bags integrity-protected only by the standardized publicly-known `"atakatak"` password, so the TLS channel is the only real confidentiality boundary on that endpoint. EST (RFC 7030) endpoints (`/.well-known/est/*`) share the same listener, so the precondition check implicitly refuses plaintext EST as well — if the TLS config is rejected, nothing starts. Separately, `rustgate_enrollment::ratelimit::RateLimiter::check` now fails closed on state-mutex poison (issue #767 / F-41): the previous `Err(e) => e.into_inner()` path trusted potentially corrupted map state on the security-critical CSR-signing hot path and could bypass the per-IP rate limit if a prior panic had left the sweep counters in an inconsistent state. The new posture returns `false` (handler translates to HTTP 503), fires the optional `PoisonCallback`, and emits a WARN-level `tracing` record; poison is sticky with no in-process recovery. A new `--features metrics` counter `rustgate_enrollment_rate_limit_poison_total` exposes poison events as a distinct metric from rate-limit rejections (both surface as 503 otherwise), so an alert on any non-zero sample catches the degraded state quickly. Pinned by three new regression tests: `poisoned_mutex_fails_closed_and_fires_callback` and `poisoned_mutex_without_callback_still_fails_closed` in `ratelimit.rs`, and `run_rejects_empty_cert_pem_to_prevent_plaintext` / `run_rejects_empty_key_pem_to_prevent_plaintext` in `server_integration.rs`. (#767)
- **Persist the CRL `crlNumber` counter across enrollment-server restarts** — `rustgate_enrollment::RevocationStore` previously held the CRL serial in a `static AtomicU64` initialised to `1` at process start, so every restart re-issued a CRL with `crlNumber = 1`. RFC 5280 §5.2.3 requires the `crlNumber` extension to increase monotonically for each CRL issued by a given CA, and x509 verifiers that enforce this (OpenSSL's `X509_CRL_get_ext_d2i(…, NID_crl_number)` path among them) will reject a restarted-CA's CRL as a replay of a prior one — locking clients into stale revocation state until their cached CRL expires, or hard-failing verification depending on verifier posture. The counter now lives on the store instance (`RevocationStore::crl_serial: AtomicU64`) and is persisted to the revocation JSON under a new versioned wrapper (`StoredDb { format_version, last_crl_number, entries }`); the legacy pre-#765 flat-map layout still loads via a serde `untagged` fallback, so existing deployments upgrade transparently on the first persist. When no prior counter is on disk (fresh install, lost state file, or a legacy document), the in-memory counter seeds from `wall_clock_fallback_serial` (UNIX milliseconds) so the next `crlNumber` exceeds any plausible small-integer serial an older build would have issued — preserving monotonicity even across state loss. A hand-crafted or corrupted `last_crl_number = 0` is treated identically to "no stored value" (filtered out before the fallback) so defence-in-depth survives a truncated-but-valid-looking file. The `revoke` hot path now regenerates the CRL **before** `persist`, capturing the bumped serial atomically with the new revoked entry; the initial CRL issued in `RevocationStore::new` is followed by an explicit `persist` so a crash between startup and the first revocation cannot re-issue the same `crlNumber`. Pinned by four new tests in `revocation.rs`: `crl_serial_seeds_from_wall_clock_when_db_missing`, `crl_serial_persists_and_grows_across_reload` (the direct #765 regression guard), `crl_serial_increments_per_revocation`, and `crl_serial_accepts_legacy_flat_db_and_applies_fallback` (upgrade path), plus `crl_serial_zero_is_treated_as_missing` for the hand-crafted-file corner case. (#765)
- **Zero the CA private key's serialized DER on enrollment-server teardown** — `rustgate-enrollment`'s `CsrSigner` and `RevocationStore` previously held the CA key in a bare `rcgen::KeyPair`, which does not implement `Zeroize`. rcgen internally keeps two copies of the private key material: a ring-backed parsed form in `KeyPair::kind` and a plain `Vec<u8>` copy of the PKCS#8 DER retained for re-serialization via `KeyPair::serialize_der`. On `EnrollmentServer` shutdown both copies were released to the allocator without wiping, so the PKCS#8 DER sat in freed heap until overwritten — reachable to any adversary with post-process memory access (e.g. core-dump capture, swap residue, or a co-located attacker exploiting a later allocator reuse). The workspace now enables the `zeroize` cargo feature on rcgen 0.14.7 (unlocks `impl Zeroize for KeyPair` that wipes `serialized_der`), and a new `rustgate_enrollment::keypair::ZeroizingKeyPair` newtype forces that `zeroize()` call from its `Drop` impl. `CsrSigner::issuer` and `RevocationStore::issuer` are retyped to `Issuer<'static, ZeroizingKeyPair>`; the wrapper delegates `SigningKey` and `PublicKeyData` by reference so rcgen's certificate and CRL signing paths see no behavioural change. The ring-level parsed key in `KeyPair::kind` is a known residual — ring does not expose a `Zeroize` bound on its `EcdsaKeyPair` / `Ed25519KeyPair` / `RsaKeyPair` types, and the workspace-wide `unsafe_code = "forbid"` lint rules out pointer-cast workarounds; the wrapper documents the residue explicitly instead of concealing it. A dedicated unit test calls `zeroize()` on a populated rcgen `KeyPair` and verifies `serialize_der()` subsequently returns all-zero bytes, pinning the upstream contract so a future rcgen bump that regresses `impl Zeroize` fails CI instead of silently widening the residue window. `Debug` redaction is asserted alongside so the wrapper's secret-hygiene property cannot be quietly weakened by a downstream trait-impl change. (#766)
- **Cap audit-chain rewind budget and enforce minimum archive rotation cadence** — closes both halves of #811. **F-32**: `rustgate_core::audit_chain::read_chain_tip` previously read the entire audit log into memory via `file.read_to_end(&mut bytes)?` at every startup and, on any torn-line recovery, recursed by re-opening and re-reading the whole file from scratch — a hostile or accidentally-enormous log (e.g. 10 GiB of garbage) could stall the tip-recovery phase for minutes and, with each corrupt trailing record, walk the whole file again, compounding the delay into an effective startup DoS. The function is now refactored to seek to `file_len.saturating_sub(REWIND_BUDGET)` (16 MiB) and read only the trailing window, drops any partial leading record that straddles the budget boundary, and walks torn/corrupt trailing records in a bounded iterative loop (not recursive) whose worst-case work is `O(REWIND_BUDGET)` regardless of file size. If the entire rewound tail contains no valid record — i.e. the tip is genuinely unrecoverable — the function returns `io::ErrorKind::InvalidData` instead of silently re-genesising the chain, so the operator must verify/repair the log out-of-band; a tamper-detection posture rather than a tamper-hiding one.
